Geometric approach to stable homotopy groups of spheres II; Arf-Kervaire Invariants
P.M. Akhmetiev

Abstract
Аннотация The Kervaire Invariant 1 Problem until recently was an open problem in algebraic topology. Hill-Hopkins-Ravenel theorem clams a negative solution of the problem for all dimensions n = 2 l − 2 n=2^{l}-2 , l ≥ 8 l\geq 8 [ H-H-R ] . In dimension n = 126 = 2 7 − 2 n=126=2^{7}-2 , the problem is open, it is known as a hypothesis by V.P. Snaith (2009). We prove the statement of Hill-Hopkins-Ravenel theorem for all dimensions 2 l − 2 2^{l}-2 , l ≥ l 0 l\geq l_{0} , where l 0 l_{0} is a sufficiently great positive integer. We prove that there is no element in Π 126 \Pi_{126} with Kervaire invariant one which is desuspended 9 9 times into the homotopy group π 126 + 128 − 9 ​ ( S 128 − 9 ) \pi_{126+128-9}(S^{128-9}) . The proof is based on the Hirsh control principle and the compression Theorem. A notion internal symmetry: of Abelian (for skew-framed immersions), bi-cyclic (for ℤ / 2 [ 3 ] {\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]} -framed immersions) and quaternion-cyclic structure (for ℤ / 2 [ 4 ] {\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]} -framed immersions) are introduced.



1
Self-intersections of generic immersions and the Kervaire invariant; the problem statement

The Kervaire Invariant 1 Problem until recently was an open problem in algebraic topology. In dimension

n=126
, the problem has not been solved and has the status of a hypothesis by
V.P. Snaith (2009)
[
S
]
.
A geometrical approach toward the solution is proposed. The author assumes that the Arf-Kervaire invariant one Problem in all dimensions, including the dimension
126
, will be solved, because additional assumptions concerning compression (see Main Result in the Introduction) could be presented precisely. Our approach develops an approach by L.S.Pontryagin
[
P
]
,
and by V.A.Rokhlin
[
Ro
]
.

Let us consider a smooth generic immersion

f:M^{n-1}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

n=2^{\ell}-2
,
\ell>1
of the codimension
1
. Denote by

g:N^{n-2}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
the immersion of self-intersection manifold of
f
.

Let us recall a definition of the cobordism group

Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)
, a particular case
k=1
is better known:

Imm^{sf}(n-1,1)
.
The cobordism group is defined as equivalent classes of triples up to the standard
cobordism relation, equipped with a disjoint union operation;

\bullet

f:M^{n-k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a codimension
k
immersion;

\bullet

\Xi:\nu(f)\cong k\varkappa_{M}^{\ast}(\gamma)
is a bundle map, which is invertible,
an invertible bundle map is a fibrewized isomorphism;

\bullet

\varkappa_{M}\in H^{1}(M^{n-k};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)
is a prescribed cohomology class, which is
a mapping
M^{n-k}\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{\infty}=K({\mathbb{Z}}/2,1)
.

By
\nu(f)
is denoted the normal bundle of the immersion
f
, by
\varkappa_{M}^{\ast}(\gamma)
is denoted the pull-back of
the universal line bundle
\gamma
over
{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{\infty}
by the mapping
\varkappa_{M}
,
by
k\varkappa_{M}^{\ast}(\gamma)
is denoted the Whitney sum of the
k
copies of the line bundles,
below for short we will write
k\varkappa_{M}
. The isomorphism
\Xi
is called a skew-framing
of the immersion
f
.

The Kervaire invariant is an invariant of cobordism classes, which is homomorphism

\displaystyle\Theta^{sf}:Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2.

(1)

Let us recall the homomorphism
(\ref{1a})
in the case
k=1
.

The normal bundle

\nu_{g}
of the immersion
g:N^{n-2}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

is a
2
-dimensional bundle over

N^{n-2}
, which is equipped by a
{\bf D}
–framing
\Xi
, where
{\bf D}
is the dihedral
group of the order
8
. The classifying map of this bundle (and also the corresponding characteristic class) is denoted by

\eta_{N}:N^{n-2}\to K({\bf D},1)
.
Triples

(g,\eta_{N},\Xi)
up to a cobordism relation represents an element in the group

Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2,2)
. The correspondence
(f,\varkappa,\Psi)\mapsto(g,\eta_{N},\Xi)

defines a homomorphism

\displaystyle\delta^{{\bf D}}:Imm^{sf}(n-1,1)\to Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2,2).

(2)

Definition 1
.

The Kervaire invariant of an immersion

f
is defined by the formula:

\displaystyle\Theta_{sf}(f)=\langle\eta_{N}^{\frac{n-2}{2}};[N^{n-2}]\rangle.

(3)

It is not difficult to prove that the formula

(\ref{arf})
determines a homomorphism

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\bf D}}:Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2,2)\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2.

(4)

The homomorphism
(
4
) is called the Kervaire invariant of a

{\bf D}
–framed immersion.
The composition of the homomorphisms
(\ref{2a})
,
(\ref{3a})

determines the homomorphism
(\ref{1a})
.

Main Result

Assume an element
x\in Imm^{sf}(n-1,1)

admits a compression of the order
7
(see Definition
4
).
Then in the case
n=2^{\ell}-2
,
\ell\geq 7
, the homomorphism
\Theta^{sf}
(
3
) on
x
is trivial:
\Theta^{sf}(x)=0
.

Remark 2
.

In the case
\ell=7
the statement of Main Result + a compression assumption imply the positive solution of the
Snaith Conjecture
[
S
]
. The Snaith Conjecture is an open conjecture, because if an arbitrary element
x\in Imm^{sf}(125,1)
admits a compression of the order
7
is unknown.
A result, which implies a positive solution of the Conjecture, is proved
[
R-L
]
. The same time, a negative solution of Snath Conjecture is announced in
[
M2
]
.

Main Result with an assumption
\ell\geq 8
is a corollary of
[
H-H-R
]
. If an arbitrary element
x
,
\ell\geq 8
, admits a compression of the order
7
is unknown.
Main Result and the following Compression Theorem gives an alternative proof of Hill-Hopkins-Ravenel theorem for all
n\geq 256
, probably, except a finite number of exceptional cases.

A short proof of Main Result

Main Result is proved in Theorem
30
, where the Definition (
17
) of standardized
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion is used. The standardized immersion is a result of a Smale-Hirsh control principle
[
Hi
]
for iterated self-intersection points of a standardized
{\bf D}
-framed immersion (Subsection
2.2
). The Arf-invariant for
standardized
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion is determined by a homology class, described in
Lemma
12
and then by the the middle (exceptional) class in
Definition
17
. ∎

Compression Theorem
[
Akh
]

For an arbitrary positive integer
d
there exists
\ell=\ell(d)
, such that an arbitrary
element in
Imm^{sf}(n-1,1)
admits a compression of the order
d
.

Let us explain steps in the proof Main Theorem.
Definition of the cobordism group

Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2,2)
of immersions with a dihedral framing is standard and analogous to the above definition of the skew-framed cobordism group
Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)
for
k=1
. This group also admits a standard generalization as the cobordism group
Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2k,2k)
with a parameter
k\geq 1
.
Each element in

Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2k,2k)
is represented by a triple
(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
, where

g:N^{n-2k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an immersion,

\Psi
– is a dihedral framing in the codimension
2k
,
i.e. an isomorphism

\Xi:\nu_{g}\cong k\eta_{N}^{\ast}(\tau)
, where
\eta_{N}:N\to K({\bf D},1)

is the classifying mapping, the universal
2
-dimensional
{\bf D}
-bundle over
K({\bf D},1)
is denoted by

\tau
.
The mapping

\eta_{N}
determines a dihedral framing of the normal bundle

\nu_{g}
by
\Xi
.

The Kervaire homomorphism is defined in a more general case using the following generalizations of the
homomorphisms
(
1
) and (
4
):

\displaystyle\Theta^{sf}_{k}:Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2,\qquad\Theta^{sf}_{k}:=\Theta^{{\bf D}}_{k}\circ\delta^{{\bf D}}_{k}.

(5)

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\bf D}}_{k}:Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2k,2k)\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2,\qquad\Theta^{{\bf D}}_{k}[(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})]=\langle\eta_{N}^{\frac{n-2k}{2}};[N^{n-2k}]\rangle.

(6)

In the case

k=1
the homomorphism (
6
) coincides with
the homomorphism
(
4
) above, the following commutative diagram, in which the homomorphisms
J^{sf}
,
J^{{\bf D}}

are known, is well defined.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccc}Imm^{sf}(n-1,1)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta^{{\bf D}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2,2)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Theta^{{\bf D}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&{\mathbb{Z}}/2\\
\downarrow J^{sf}_{k}&&\downarrow J^{{\bf D}}_{k}&&||\\
Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta^{{\bf D}}_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2k,2k)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Theta^{{\bf D}}_{k}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&{\mathbb{Z}}/2.\\
\end{array}

Let us remind, that an idea to investigate intersection points as in Theorem
22

was communicated to me by A.V.Chernavskii (1996). S.A.Melikhov (2005) has noted that a quaternion analogue
of the Chernavskii mapping as in Lemma
27
is required. An idea to make a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-control as in the formula
(
53
) was realized after a talk by E.V.Scepin (2004).
As a conclusion, results was collected-out at the A.S.Mishchenko Seminar in
2008-2024. Alexandr S. Mischenko and Theodor Yu. Popelenskii allow discussions, in particular, we will use Popelenskii’s formulas in Section
7
.

2
First step

2.1
The dihedral group
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}={\bf D}

In the present and the next sections the cobordism groups

Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)
,
Imm^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}}(n-2k,2k)
will be used.
In the case the first argument in the bracket is strongly positive, the cobordism group is finite.

The dihedral group

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}={\bf D}
is defined by its corepresentation

\{a,b\,|\,b^{4}=a^{2}=e,[a,b]=b^{2}\}.

This group is represented by rotations a subgroup in the group
O(2)
of orthogonal transformation of the
standard plane. Elements transforms the base
\{{\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2}\}
on the plane

Lin({\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2})
to itself, a non-ordered pair of coordinate lines on the plane
are preserved by transformations.
The element
b
is represented by the rotation of the plane by the angle

\frac{\pi}{2}
. The element
a
is represented by the reflection of the plane
relative to the straight line

l_{1}=Lin({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{2})
parallel to the vector
{\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{2}
.

Let us consider a subgroup

{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}={\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}
in the dihedral group,
which is generated by the elements
\{a,b^{2}a\}
. This is an elementary
2
-group of the rank
2
. Transformations of this group keep each line
l_{1}
,
l_{2}
with the base vectors
{\bf f}_{1}={\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{2}
,
{\bf f}_{2}={\bf e}_{1}-{\bf e}_{2}
correspondingly.
The cohomology group
H^{1}(K({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2)
contains two generators
\varkappa_{a},\varkappa_{\dot{a}}
.

Let us define the cohomology classes

\displaystyle\varkappa_{a}\in H^{1}(K({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2)),\quad\varkappa_{\dot{a}}\in H^{1}(K({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2)).

(11)

Denote by
p_{a}:{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\to{\bf I}_{a}
a projection, the kernel of
p_{a}
consists the symmetry transformation with respect to
the bisector of the second coordinate angle and the identity.

Denote
\varkappa_{a}=p_{a}^{\ast}(t_{a})
, where
e\neq t_{a}\in H^{1}(K({\bf I}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2)\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}/2
. Let us denote by
p_{\dot{a}}:{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\to{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}
the projection, the kernel of
p_{\dot{a}}
consists of the symmetry
with respect to the bisector of the first coordinate angle and the identity.

Let us denote
\varkappa_{\dot{a}}=p_{\dot{a}}^{\ast}(t_{\dot{a}})
, where
e\neq t_{\dot{a}}\in H^{1}(K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.

2.2
A standardized immersion with a dihedral framing

Let us consider a
{\bf D}
-framed immersion

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
of codimension
2k
. Let us assume that
the image of
g
contains in a regular neighborhood
U({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2})
of the embedding

{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. The following mapping

\pi\circ\eta_{N}:N^{n-2k}\to K({\bf D},1)\to K({\mathbb{Z}}/2,1)
is well defined, where

K({\bf D},1)\to K({\mathbb{Z}}/2,1)
is the epimorphism with the kernel
{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\subset{\bf D}
.
It is required that this mapping coincides to the composition

i\circ PROJ\circ\eta_{N}
, where

PROJ:U({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2})\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
is the projection of the neighborhood onto its central line,

i:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\subset K({\mathbb{Z}}/2,1)
is the standard inclusion, which transforms the fundamental class into the generator.

2.2.1
Condition C1 for standardized immersions

Let us consider the submanifold
N_{sing}^{n-2k-2}\subset N^{n-2k}
,
N_{sing}^{n-2k-2}=PROJ^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{0}),{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
.
Require that the mapping

\eta_{N}
, restricted to

N_{sing}
, be skipped trough the skeleton of
K({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1)
of the dimension strictly less then
\frac{3n}{4}
.

Geometrically, this means that the submanifold
N_{sing}
is a defect of a reduction
of the structured mapping of the normal bundle with a control of the subgroup

{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\subset{\bf D}
over
{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
to a mapping with a control of the same type over
{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
.

In the case

k\to 1+
the structuring mapping on the defect manifold is skipped through a polyhedron of the dimension

\frac{3n}{4}
.

The complement
N\setminus N_{sing}
to the defect we get an open manifold, for which the classifying normal bundle mapping is given by

\eta_{N}:N\setminus N_{sing}\to K({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a})\rtimes_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{1},

for the involution
\chi=\chi^{[2]}
, see definition (
28
).

Definition 3
.

Let us say that a
{\bf D}
-framed immersion

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N};\Upsilon)
of the codimension
2k
is standardized by
\Upsilon
with
a defect
\delta<\frac{3n}{4}
, if the above condition
C1
is satisfied with a prescribed defect.

Quadruples
(g,\Psi,\eta_{N};\Upsilon)
, up to cobordism relations generate an abelian group

Imm^{D;stand}(n-2k,2k)
; there is natural forgetful homomorphism:

\displaystyle FG:Imm^{{\bf D};stand}(n-2k,2k)\longrightarrow Imm^{sf}(n-2k,2k),

(12)

after a control structure
\Upsilon
is omitted.

Definition 4
.

Assume
[(f,\Xi,\varkappa_{M})]\in Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)
,

f:M^{n-k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\varkappa_{M}\in H^{1}(M^{n-k};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)
,

\Xi
is a skew-framing. Let us say that the pair
(M^{n-k},\varkappa_{M})

admits a compression of an order
q
, if the mapping

\varkappa_{M}:M^{n-k}\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{\infty}
is represented (up to homotopy) by the following composition:

\varkappa=I\circ\varkappa_{M}^{\prime}:M^{n-k}\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k-q-1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{\infty}
. Let us say that the element
[(f,\Xi,\varkappa_{M})]

admits a compression of an order
q
, if in its cobordism class exists a triple

(f^{\prime},\Xi^{\prime},\varkappa_{M^{\prime}})
, which admits a compression of the order
q
.

Below we assume
n_{\sigma}=14
,
k=\frac{n-n_{\sigma}}{16}
. In particular, in the case
n=126

we get
k=7
and in the case
n=254
we get
k=15
.

Theorem 5
.

Assume that
\ell\geq 7
and that an element
x\in Imm^{sf}(n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{16},\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{16})=Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)
admits a compression of the order

q=7

By this additional assumption one may get an element
y=\delta_{k}^{{\bf D}}\in Imm^{{\bf D}}(n-2k,2k)
,
1
1

1

The homomorphism
Imm^{sf}(n-k,k)\to Imm^{sf;stand}(n-k,k)
is not claimed
such that there exist an element

z\in Imm^{sf;stand}(n-k;k)
,
z=[(g,\eta_{N};\Xi;\Upsilon)]
,
FG(z)=y
by
(\ref{for})
,
\Upsilon
is the normalized structure with an additional condition:

a submanifold
N^{n_{\sigma}}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset N^{n-2k}
,

\displaystyle N^{n_{\sigma}}_{a\times\dot{a}}=\eta_{N}^{-1}(K(\infty-2(q+1))\subset K({\bf D},1)),

(13)

where
K(\infty-2(q+1))\subset K({\bf D},1)
is a skeleton of the codimension
2(q+1)
in the
universal space
K({\bf D},1)
, determined by the Euler class of the bundle
(q+1)\tau
over
K({\bf D},1)
: the Whitney sum of
(q+1)
copies of the universal
2
-bundle
\tau
,
admits a reduction of its structured subgroup
{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\subset{\bf D}
of the normal bundle
(equivalently, the mapping
\pi\circ g:N^{n-2k}\looparrowright U({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2})\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
,
restricted on the submanifold
N^{n_{\sigma}}_{a\times\dot{a}}
, homotopic to the constant mapping).

2.2.2
A preliminary construction for Theorem
5

.

Let

\displaystyle d^{(2)}:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}_{diag}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

(14)

be an arbitrary
(T_{{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}},T_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
–equivariant mapping, which is transversal along the diagonal

{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{diag}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
The diagonal in the preimage is mapped into the diagonal of the image, by this reason the equivariant
mapping
d^{(2)}
is defined on the open manifold outside of the diagonal (in the pre-image).

(In Theorem
5

k^{\prime}
has to be defined
by the formula:
k^{\prime}=k+q+1
(parameters
k
,
q
correspond to denotations of Theorem

5
).)

Let us re-denote

(d^{(2)})^{-1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{diag})/T_{{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}}
by
{\bf N}_{\circ}={\bf N}(d^{(2)})_{\circ}
for short,
this polyhedron is called a polyhedron of (formal) self-intersection of the equivariant mapping
d^{(2)}
.

The polyhedron
{\bf N}_{\circ}
is an open polyhedron, this polyhedron admits a compactification, which is denoted by
{\bf N}
with a boundary
\partial{\bf N}
. The boundary consists of all critical (formal critical) points of the
mapping
d^{(2)}
. One has:

{\bf N}\setminus\partial{\bf N}={\bf N}_{\circ}
. Let us denote by
U(\partial{\bf N})_{\circ}
a
thin regular neighborhood of the boundary (= a diagonal)

\partial{\bf N}
.

The polyhedron
{\bf N}_{\circ}
is equipped by the mapping
d^{(2)}
,
which admits the following lift:

\displaystyle\eta_{Ab}:{\bf N}_{\circ}\to K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1).

(15)

The relation between the mapping
d^{(2)}
, called the structured mapping, and its lift (
15
) is the following:

\displaystyle\eta_{\circ}:{\bf N}_{\circ}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{Ab}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}),{\mathbb{Z}},1)\longrightarrow K({\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]},1).

(16)

On the polyhedron
U(\partial{\bf N})_{\circ}
the mapping
\eta_{circ;Ab}

gets the values into the following subcomplex:

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\times 2{\mathbb{Z}},1)\subset K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)
(the projection on
K({\mathbb{Z}},1)=S^{1}
is the analogue of the projection of the Moebius band onto its central line).

Definition 6
.

Let us call a formal (equivariant) mapping

d^{(2)}
, given by
(\ref{d2})
, is holonomic, if this mapping
is the formal extension of a mapping

\displaystyle d:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

(17)

Definition 7
.

Assume a formal (equivariant) mapping
(\ref{d2})
is holonomic.
Let us say
d^{(2)}
admits an Abelian structure, if the following two conditions are satisfied.

– 1. On the open polyhedron
{\bf N}_{\circ}
the mapping (
15
)
is well defined,
which is a lift of the structured mapping (
16
),
i.e. the composition of the lift

\eta_{Ab}
with a natural mapping

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}){\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)\to K({\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]},1)

coincides with the structured mapping
(
16
).

–2. Let us consider the Moebius band
M^{2}

and represent the Eilenberg-MacLane space
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)

as a skew-product
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\tilde{\times}M^{2}\to M^{2}
; the restriction of the bundle over
the boundary circle

S^{1}=\partial(M^{2})
is identified with the subspace
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\times 2{\mathbb{Z}},1)\subset K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)
.
Let us include the space
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\tilde{\times}M^{2}
into

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\tilde{\times}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
by a gluing of the trivial bundle over
the boundary
S^{1}
by the trivial bundle over a small disk
with a central point
{\bf x}_{\infty}\in{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
.

The
resulting space is denoted by

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
. The following mapping:

\displaystyle\eta_{\circ;Ab}:({\bf N}_{\circ},U(\partial{\bf N}_{\circ}))\to(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2},K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\times{\bf x}_{\infty}),

(18)

is well defined, where the inverse image by
\eta_{\circ;Ab}
of a layer

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\times{\bf y}_{\infty}\subset K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
, (
{\bf y}_{\infty}\approx{\bf x}_{\infty}
) is dominated by a subpolyhedron of the dimension

\frac{n-3k^{\prime}}{2}=\frac{{\operatorname{dim}}({\bf N}_{\circ})}{2}-k^{\prime}

(up to a small constant
d=2
), which is equipped by the structured mapping.

Remark 8
.

The condition –2 in Definition
7

can be formulated, alternatively, as following: in the polyhedron
{\bf N}_{\circ}
there exists a subpolyhedron

{\bf N}_{\circ;reg}\subset U(\partial{\bf N}_{\circ})\subset{\bf N}_{\circ}
,
which is inside its proper thin neighborhood,
and there exists a polyhedron
P
of the dimension (up to a small constant
d=2
)

3
Local coefficients and homology groups

Let us define the group

({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}){\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}
and the epimorphism

({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}){\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}.

Consider the automorphism

\displaystyle\chi^{[2]}:{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\to{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}

(27)

of the exterior conjugation of the subgroup

{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\subset{\bf D}
by the element
ba\in{\bf D}
,
this element is represented by the reflection of the plane with respect to the line
Lin({\bf e}_{2})
.
Let us define the automorphism (denotations are not changed)

\displaystyle\chi^{[2]}:{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]},

(28)

by permutations of the base vectors.
It is not difficult to check, that the inclusion

{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}
commutes with automorphisms

(\ref{chidd})
,
(\ref{chi2})
in the image and the preimage.

Define the group

\displaystyle{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}.

(29)

Let us consider the quotient
of the group
{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\ast{\mathbb{Z}}
(the free product of the group

{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}
) by the relation
zxz^{-1}=\chi^{[2]}(x)
, where
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}

is the generator,

x\in{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
is an arbitrary element.

This group is a particular example of a semi-direct product
A\rtimes_{\phi}B
,
A={\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
,
B={\mathbb{Z}}
, by a homomorphism
\phi:B\to Aut(A)
; the set
A\times B

is equipped with a binary operation
(a_{1},b_{1})\ast(a_{2},b_{2})\mapsto(a_{1}\phi_{b_{1}}(b_{2}),b_{1}b_{2})
.
Let us define the group

(\ref{chi2Z})
by this construction for
A={\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
,
B={\mathbb{Z}}
,

\phi=\chi^{[2]}
.

The classifying space
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)
is a skew-product over the circle
S^{1}
with
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
, where the shift mapping in the cyclic covering

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\to K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
over
K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)
is induced by the automorphism
\chi^{[2]}
.
The projection onto the circle is denoted by

\displaystyle p_{a\times\dot{a}}:K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)\to S^{1}.

(30)

Take a marked point

pt_{S^{1}}\in S^{1}
and define the subspace

\displaystyle K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\subset K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)

(31)

as the inverse image of the marked point

pt_{S^{1}}
by the mapping
(\ref{pbb})
.

A description of the standard base of the group

H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})

is sufficiently complicated and is not required.
The group

H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})
is described using the Kunneth formula:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}0\to\bigoplus_{i_{1}+i_{2}=i}H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf I}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i_{1}+i_{2}=i-1}Tor^{{\mathbb{Z}}}(H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf I}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}}),H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\to 0.\\
\end{array}

The standard base of the group

H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1))
contains the following elements:

x\otimes y/(x\otimes y)-(y\otimes x),

where
x\in H_{j}(K({\bf I}_{a},1))
,
y\in H_{i-j}(K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1))

(
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
–coefficients is the formulas are omitted).

In particular, for odd
i
the group

H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})

contains elements, which are defined by the fundamental classes
of the following submanifolds:

{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{i}\times pt\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{i}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{i}\subset K({\bf I}_{a},1)\times K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1)=K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
,
pt\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{i}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{i}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{i}\subset K({\bf I}_{a},1)\times K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1)=K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
.
Let us denote the corresponding elements as following:

\displaystyle t_{a,i}\in H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1);{\mathbb{Z}}),t_{\dot{a},i}\in H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1);{\mathbb{Z}}).

(36)

The following analogues of the homology groups

H_{i}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1))
,

\displaystyle p_{a\times\dot{a}}:{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}

x\ast y\mapsto y
,

p_{a\times\dot{a}}\pmod{2}

Let us define the group
(\ref{Zlok})

.
Because all non-trivial homology classes of the space

A subgroup

\displaystyle D_{i}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)\subset H_{i}^{loc}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2)

Lemma 10
.

The group
H_{2s}^{loc}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})
contains a direct factor by the subgroup

D_{2s}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}})
. The elements from the subgroup

\bigoplus_{i_{1}+i_{2}=2s}H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf I}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\subset D_{2s}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a};{\mathbb{Z}}),

is mapped monomorphic into the image
\mathrm{Im}(A)
by the following homomorphism:

\displaystyle A:H_{2s}^{loc}(K({\bf I}_{a\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\to

(45)

H_{2s}^{loc}(K({\bf I}_{a\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}}/2),

which is the modulo 2 reduction of the coefficient system.

Proof of Lemma
10

Assume an element
x\otimes y\in H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf I}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1);{\mathbb{Z}})

is a cycle in
H_{2s}^{loc}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})
, which is represented by a singular manifold
f:V^{2s}\longrightarrow K({\bf I}_{a},1)\times K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1)
. Take the boundary

W^{2s+1}
,
\partial W^{2s+1}=V^{2s}
and consider the cycle

F:(W^{2s+1},V^{2s})\longrightarrow(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1),K({\bf I}_{a},1)\times K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1))

with a local coefficient system, which is given by the collection of a paths from centers of simplexes to the marked point in the subspace of the pair. We may assume, that the singular cycle

F
is such that the manifold is catted by the hypermanifold
U^{2s}\subset W^{2s+1}
, the
local path system at the two components of the boundary of
the cutted submanifold
W^{2s-1}\setminus U^{2s}
have no intersection points of paths with
U^{2s}
.
Then, the two copies
U_{+}^{2s}
,
U^{2s}_{-}
of the boundary are represented by
\chi^{(2)}
-conjugated cycles and after the homotopy of
U^{2s}\subset W^{2s+1}
into a subspace

K({\bf I}_{a},1)\times K({\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1)\subset K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)
we get
an ordinary singular cycle with 3 components of boundary:
V^{2s}
,
U^{2s}
and
-U^{2s}
,
where
-\dots
is denoted the cycle with the opposite orientation. Lemma
10
is proved. ∎

Let us consider the diagonal subgroup:

i_{{\bf I}_{d},{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}}:{\bf I}_{d}\subset{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}={\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
.
This subgroup coincides to the kernel of the homomorphism

\displaystyle\omega^{[2]}:{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2,

(46)

which is defined by the formula

(x\times y)\mapsto xy
.

Let us define the homomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi^{[2]}:{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}

(47)

by the formula:

\Phi^{[2]}(z)=ab
,
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}
is the generator (the element
ab
is represented by the inversion of the first basis vector
{\mathbb{Z}}^{[2]}\subset O(2)
),

\Phi^{[2]}|_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\times\{0\}}:{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset{\bf D}

is the standard inclusion;

\Phi^{[2]}|_{z^{-1}{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}z}:{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset{\bf D}

is the conjugated inclusion, by the composition with the exterior automorphism
in the subgroup
{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}
.

Let us define

(\Phi^{[2]})^{\ast}(\tau_{[2]})=\tau_{a\times\dot{a}},

where

\tau_{a\times\dot{a}}\in H^{2}(K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1))
,
\tau_{[2]}\in H^{2}(K({\mathbb{Z}}^{[2]},1))
.

4
The fundamental class of the canonical covering over the self-intersection
D
-framed standardized immersion without a defect

Let us consider

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}
–framed immersion

The mapping
\eta_{N}
admits a reduction, the image of this mapping belongs to the total bundle space over

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)

N^{n-2k}

{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}

\displaystyle PROJ^{-1}({\bf x}_{\infty})\in N^{n-2k}.

(48)

g

,
over this point the defect is welldefined.

,

, which is a layer over the point

Let us define the lift of the characteristic class

\eta_{N,\ast}

and define the class

\displaystyle\eta_{N,\ast}([N^{n-2k}])\in H_{n-2k}^{loc}(({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\rtimes_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1);{\mathbb{Z}}).

(49)

Then let us prove that

.

) satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle\eta_{N,\ast}([N^{n-2k}])\in D_{n-2k}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}}),

).

4.0.1
Construction of the class (
49
)

Let us consider a skeleton of the space

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
, which is realized as the structured group

{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset O(1+1)
of a bundle
over the corresponding Grasmann manifold

Gr_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}}(1+1,n)
of non-oriented
1+1=2
-plans in
n
–space.
Denote this Grasmann manifold by

\displaystyle KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}})\cong K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1).

(51)

On the space
(\ref{KK1})
a free involution

\displaystyle\chi^{[2]}:KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}})\to KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}),

(52)

acts, this involution corresponds to the automorphism

(\ref{chidd})
and represents by a permutation of line of layers (by the antidiagonal matrix).

Let us define a family of spaces
(
51
) with the prescribed symmetry group, are parametrized over the base
S^{1}
,
the generator of
S^{1}
transforms layers by the involution (
27
).
This space is a subspace (a skeleton):

K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\subset K({\bf D},1)
.

Define the required space

\displaystyle KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\rtimes_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2})

(53)

by a quotient of the direct product

KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}})\times S^{2}
with respect to the involution
\chi^{[2]}\times(-1)
,
where
-1:S^{2}\to S^{2}
–is the antipodal involution.
The following inclusion is well defined:

\displaystyle KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}})\subset KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}})\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}.

(54)

The involution
(\ref{chiK})
induces the
S^{1}
-fibrewise involution

\displaystyle\chi^{[2]}:KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}})\to KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}),

(55)

which is extended to the involution

\displaystyle\chi^{[2]}:KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\rtimes_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2})\to KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\rtimes_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2})

(56)

on
KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}})\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
,
all this extended involutions denote the same.

The universal
2
-dimensional

{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}/2
–bundle
over the target space in (
54
)

KK({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
, which is denoted by

\tau_{a\times\dot{a},\rtimes}
,
is well defined.
We will need mostly the restriction of this bundle to the subspace (
53
).
The restriction of this bundle on the origin
subspace of
(\ref{KK1})
is the involutive pull-back of the universal
2
-bundle
\tau_{a\times\dot{a}}
over the target space of
(\ref{KK1})
.

The first direct factor of the
{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}
-framing of the immersion

by

, determines the Gaussean mapping in (

). The restriction of this mapping on (

\frac{3n}{4}
-skeleton of the subspace (

4.0.2
Proof of Theorem
11

Let us prove that the class (
49
) satisfies the equation (
50
).
Take the decomposition (
21
). The first manifold in the right hand-side of the formula is mapped into the skeleton of the dimension
n-2k-2q
, where
q=7
. Therefore the fundamental class of the second term gives a contribution to the characteristic class (
49
). By construction, the formula (
50
) is satisfied. Theorem
11
is proved. ∎

In the case
dim(N)=n-2k
, we get
dim(\bar{N}^{n_{\sigma}}_{a\times\dot{a}})=n_{\sigma}=n-16k
, see (
13
), Theorem
5
;
recall, in the case
n=126
one gets
k=7
and
n_{\sigma}=14
.

Let us consider the element

\displaystyle\eta_{\ast}(N^{n_{\sigma}}_{a\times\dot{a}})\in D_{n_{\sigma}}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}}).

(57)

Let us prove that the element (
57
) satisfies an additional property, which is called pure standardized Property, Definition
13
below.

Lemma 12
.

–1. The element
(\ref{etadlok})

is decomposed with respect to the standard base of the group

H_{n_{\sigma}}(K({\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a},1))
contains not more then the only monomial

t_{a,i}\otimes t_{\dot{a},i}
, see.
(\ref{tdtdd})
,
i=\frac{n_{\sigma}}{2}=\frac{n-16k}{2}
.
The coefficient of this monomial coincides with the Kervaire invariant, which is calculated for
a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}
–framed immersion
(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
.

Proof of Lemma
12

Let us proof the statement for the case

n_{\sigma}=14
. Let us consider the manifold
N_{a\times\dot{a}}^{n_{\sigma}}
,
which we re-denote in the proof by

N^{14}
for sort. This manifold is equipped with the mapping

\eta:N^{14}\to K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
.
Let us consider all the collection of characteristic
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-numbers
for the mapping
\eta
, which is induced from the universal classes.
The Hurewitcz image
\eta_{\ast}([N^{14}])
is in
the group
D_{n_{\sigma}}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)
.

Let us estimate the fundamental class

[N^{14}]
and prove that this class is pure (see definition
13
) below. Because

N^{14}
is oriented, among characteristic numbers:

\varkappa_{a}\varkappa^{13}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}^{3}\varkappa^{11}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}^{5}\varkappa^{9}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}^{7}\varkappa^{7}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}^{9}\varkappa^{5}_{\dot{a}}
,

\varkappa_{a}^{3}\varkappa^{11}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}\varkappa^{13}_{\dot{a}}
.
the only number
\varkappa_{a}^{7}\varkappa^{7}_{\dot{a}}
could be nontrivial.

Let us prove that the characteristic number

\varkappa_{a}\varkappa^{13}_{\dot{a}}
is trivial. Let us consider the manifold

K^{3}\subset N^{14}
, which is dual to the characteristic class
\varkappa_{a}\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{10}
. The normal bundle

\nu_{N}
over the manifold
N^{14}
is isomorphic to the Whitney sum
k\varkappa_{a}\oplus k\varkappa_{\dot{a}}
, where the positive integer
k
satisfies the equation:

k\equiv 0\pmod{8}
.
The restriction of the bundle

\nu_{N}
on the submanifold
K^{3}
is trivial.
Therefore, the normal bundle

\nu_{K}
of the manifold
K^{3}
is stably isomorphic to the bundle

\varkappa_{a}\oplus 2\varkappa_{\dot{a}}
.

Because the characteristic class

w_{2}(K^{3})
is trivial we get:

\langle\varkappa^{3}_{\dot{a}};[K^{3}]\rangle=0
. But,
\langle\varkappa^{3}_{\dot{a}};[K^{3}]\rangle=\langle\varkappa_{a}\varkappa^{13}_{\dot{a}};[N^{14}]\rangle
.
This proves that the characteristic number

\varkappa_{a}\varkappa^{13}_{\dot{a}}
is trivial.
Analogously, the characteristic numbers

\varkappa_{a}^{5}\varkappa^{9}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}^{9}\varkappa^{5}_{\dot{a}}
,
\varkappa_{a}\varkappa^{13}_{\dot{a}}
are trivial.

Let us prove that the characteristic number

\varkappa_{a}^{3}\varkappa^{11}_{\dot{a}}
is trivial. Let us consider the submanifold

K^{7}\subset N^{14}
, which is dual to the characteristic class

\varkappa_{a}^{2}\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{5}
. The normal bundle
\nu_{K}
of the manifold
K^{6}

is stably isomorphic to the Whitney sum

2\varkappa_{a}\oplus 5\varkappa_{\dot{a}}
. Because
w_{5}(K^{7})=0
,
the characteristic class

\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{5}
is trivial. In particular,

\langle\varkappa_{a}\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{6};[K^{7}]\rangle=0
.
The following equation is satisfied:

\langle\varkappa_{a}\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{6};[K^{7}]\rangle=\langle\varkappa_{a}^{3}\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{11};[N^{14}]\rangle
.

It is proved that the characteristic number

\varkappa_{a}^{3}\varkappa^{11}_{\dot{a}}
is trivial. Analogously,
the characteristic number

\varkappa_{a}^{11}\varkappa^{3}_{\dot{a}}
is trivial.

It is sufficiently to note, that the characteristic number

\langle\varkappa_{a}^{7}\varkappa_{\dot{a}}^{7};[N^{14}]\rangle
coincides with the
characteristic number (
6
) in the lemma.
Lemma
12
is proved. ∎

Definition 13
.

Let
(g,\eta_{N},\Psi;\Upsilon)
be the standardized
{\bf D}
-framed immersion in the codimension
2k
.
Let us say that this standardized immersion is negligible, if its fundamental class

\pmod{2}
is in the subgroup

\displaystyle\eta_{\ast}(N^{n-2k}_{a\times\dot{a}})\in D_{n-2k}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)

(58)

and is trivial.

Let us say that this standardized immersion is pure, if its fundamental class

\pmod{2}
satisfies the condition of Lemma

12
, i.e. the Hurewich image of the fundamental class is in the group

\in D_{n-2k}^{loc}({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)
and contains no monomials

t_{a,\frac{n-2k}{2}+i}\otimes t_{\dot{a},\frac{n-2k}{2}-i}
,
i\in\{\pm 1;\pm 2;\pm 3;\pm 4;\pm 5;\pm 6;\pm 7\}
, but, probably, contains the monomial

t_{a,\frac{n-2k}{2}}\otimes t_{\dot{a},\frac{n-2k}{2}}
.

We may reformulate results by Lemma
12
using Definition
13
.

Proposition 14
.

Assume an element
[(g,\eta_{N},\Psi)]\in Imm^{D}(n-2k,2k)
is in the image of the vertical
homomorphism in the diagram
(\ref{5a})
. Then this element is represented by a standardized immersion, which is pure in the sense of Definition
13
.

5
{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}
–structure on
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]}
–framed immersion

{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
–structure on

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
–framed immersion

The group

{\bf I}_{b}
is defined as the cyclic subgroup of the order

4
in the dihedral group:

{\bf I}_{b}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}
.

Let us define the subgroup

\displaystyle i_{\tilde{{\bf J}}_{b}\times\tilde{{\dot{\bf J}}}_{b}}:\tilde{{\bf J}}_{b}\times\tilde{{\dot{\bf J}}}_{b}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]},

(59)

each group
\tilde{{\bf J}}_{b}
,
\tilde{{\dot{\bf J}}}_{b}
is the order
8
group, which is isomorphic to the Cartesian product of the two cyclic groups
{\mathbb{Z}}/4\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
.

The subgroup (
60
) admits a subgroup with the splitted projection onto:

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bf J}}_{b}\times\tilde{{\dot{\bf J}}}_{b}\supset{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b},

(60)

each group
{\bf J}_{b}
,
{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
is the cyclic
4
group.

The group
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
(the monodromy group for the
3
-uple iterated self-intersection
of a skew-framed immersion) is defined using the base
({\bf e}_{1},\dots,{\bf e}_{4},{\bf f}_{1},\dots,{\bf f}_{4})
in
the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{8}
. Let us denote the order
4
generators of the subgroup

{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
by
b
,
{\dot{b}}
correspondingly.
The generators of the order
2
in the extended group
\tilde{{\bf J}}_{b}\times\tilde{{\dot{\bf J}}}_{b}
are denoted by
d
,
\dot{d}
correspondingly.

Let us describe transformations
in

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
, which corresponds to each generator.
Let us consider two subspaces described by the base vectors as following:

\displaystyle Lin_{{\bf x}}=\{{\bf e}_{1},\dots,{\bf e}_{4}\},\quad Lin_{{\bf y}}=\{{\bf f}_{1},\dots,{\bf f}_{4}\}.

(61)

The linear space
Lin_{{\bf x}}\oplus Lin_{{\bf y}}
is equipped with the
{\mathbb{Z}}/4\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
action by the generators
(b,d)
:

b({\bf e}_{1})={\bf e}_{2};\ b({\bf e}_{2})=-{\bf e}_{1},\ b({\bf e}_{3})={\bf e}_{4};\ b({\bf e}_{4})=-{\bf e}_{3};

b({\bf f}_{1})={\bf f}_{3};\ b({\bf f}_{2})={\bf f}_{4},\ b({\bf f}_{3})={\bf f}_{1};\ b({\bf f}_{4})=-{\bf f}_{2};

d({\bf e}_{1})=-{\bf e}_{1};\ d({\bf e}_{2})={\bf e}_{2},\ d({\bf e}_{3})=-{\bf e}_{3};\ d({\bf e}_{4})={\bf e}_{4};

d({\bf f}_{1})={\bf f}_{1};\ d({\bf f}_{2})={\bf f}_{2},\ d({\bf f}_{3})={\bf f}_{3};\ d({\bf f}_{4})={\bf f}_{4}.

The linear subpace
Lin_{{\bf x}}\oplus Lin_{{\bf y}}
is equipped with the
{\mathbb{Z}}/4\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2
action by the generators
({\dot{b}},\dot{d})
:

{\dot{b}}({\bf e}_{1})={\bf f}_{3};\ {\dot{b}}({\bf e}_{3})={\bf f}_{1},\ {\dot{b}}({\bf e}_{2})={\bf f}_{4};\ {\dot{b}}({\bf e}_{4})={\bf f}_{2};

{\dot{b}}({\bf f}_{1})={\bf f}_{2};\ {\dot{b}}({\bf f}_{2})=-{\bf f}_{1},\ {\dot{b}}({\bf f}_{3})={\bf f}_{4};\ {\dot{b}}({\bf f}_{4})=-{\bf f}_{3};

\dot{d}({\bf e}_{1})={\bf f}_{1};\ \dot{d}({\bf e}_{2})={\bf e}_{2},\ \dot{d}({\bf e}_{3})={\bf e}_{3};\ \dot{d}({\bf e}_{4})={\bf e}_{4};

\dot{d}({\bf f}_{1})=-{\bf f}_{1};\ \dot{d}({\bf f}_{2})={\bf f}_{2},\ \dot{d}({\bf f}_{3})=-{\bf f}_{3};\ \dot{d}({\bf f}_{4})={\bf f}_{4}.

Denote by
\chi^{[4]}:Lin_{{\bf x}}\to Lin_{{\bf y}}
the involutive isomorphism by

\chi^{[4]}({\bf e}_{1})={\bf f}_{1};\ \chi^{[4]}({\bf e}_{2})={\bf f}_{2};\ \chi^{[4]}({\bf e}_{3})={\bf f}_{3};\ \chi^{[4]}({\bf e}_{4})={\bf f}_{4}.

The automorphism is defined the blocks involution:

\chi^{[4]}:Lin_{{\bf x}}\oplus Lin_{{\bf y}}\longrightarrow Lin_{{\bf x}}\oplus Lin_{{\bf y}}.

The involution
\chi^{[4]}
satisfies the following property:

\chi^{[4]}\circ b={\dot{b}}\circ\chi^{[4]},\quad\chi^{[4]}\circ d=\dot{d}\circ\chi^{[4]}.

Let us denote the subgroup

i_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}:{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
,
which is the product of the diagonal subgroup

{\mathbb{Z}}/4\subset{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
with the elementary subgroup
{\mathbb{Z}}/2

of the second factor

{\mathbb{Z}}/2\subset{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
(or, equivalently, with the elementary subgroup
{\mathbb{Z}}/2\subset{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}

of the first factor, because the two products coinside).

The following homomorphism

\displaystyle\omega^{[4]}:{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/4,

(62)

is defined by the formula:

(x\times y)\mapsto xy
.

Let us consider the inclusion

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
, which is defined by the subspace

Lin({\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2},{\bf f}_{1},{\bf f}_{2})\subset\{{\bf e}_{1},\dots{\bf f}_{4}\}
.
The inclusion
i_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}:{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]}
is well defined, such that
the following diagram is commutative:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccc}\qquad{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{a\times\dot{a}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[2]}\\
i_{a\times\dot{a},{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}\downarrow&&i^{[3]}\downarrow\\
\qquad{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]}\\
i_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}\downarrow&&i^{[4]}\downarrow\\
\qquad{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\qquad{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}.\\
\end{array}

The following automorphisms of the order 2:

\displaystyle\chi^{[3]}:{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}\to{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},

(69)

\displaystyle\chi^{[4]}:{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\to{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b},

(70)

and

\displaystyle\chi^{[3]}:{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]},

(71)

\displaystyle\chi^{[4]}:{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]},

(72)

are marked with a loss of the strictness.

The following triple
of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-extensions of the group
{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
, defined below, is required.
The group

{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[2]}}{\mathbb{Z}}
was defined above by the formula
(\ref{chi2Z})
.
Analogously, the groups

\displaystyle{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[3]}}{\mathbb{Z}},

(73)

\displaystyle({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}){\rtimes}_{\chi^{[4]}}{\mathbb{Z}},

(74)

are defined as semi-direct products of the corresponding groups, equipped by automorphisms, with the group
{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The classifying space
K({\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[3]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)

is a skew-product of the circle

S^{1}
with the space
K({\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
, moreover, the mapping

K({\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\to K({\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)
,
which corresponds to a shift of the cyclic covering over

K({\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[3]}}{\mathbb{Z}},1)
, is defined by the involution,
which is induced by the automorphism

\chi^{[3]}
. The definition of the group
(\ref{chi4Z})
is totally analogous.

5.0.1
Laurent extensions

For the second and third lines of the diagram (
5
) let us define the primary
and secondary Laurent extensions, which are self-conjugated by
the automorphism
\chi^{[3]}
and
\chi^{[4]}
correspondingly. Let us denote the primary extension on the group
{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}
by the following bi-involutive automorphism

\displaystyle\mu^{(3)}_{b\times{\dot{b}}}=\mu_{b}^{(3)}\times\mu_{{\dot{b}}}^{(3)}:{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}\to{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}},

(75)

which inverses the cyclic generator and is given by the complex conjugation over the coordinates.

\displaystyle{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}^{(3)}}{\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

(76)

The authomorphism (
75
) is defined by the formula:

\mu_{b}^{(3)}:{\bf e}_{1}\mapsto-{\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2}\mapsto{\bf e}_{2};\qquad\mu_{{\dot{b}}}^{(3)}:{\bf f}_{1}\mapsto-{\bf f}_{1},{\bf f}_{2}\mapsto{\bf f}_{2}.

Let us denote the secondary extension with the analogous property by

\displaystyle\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}^{(4)}=\mu_{b}^{(4)}\times\mu_{{\dot{b}}}^{(4)}:{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\to{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}

(77)

The authomorphism (
77
) is defined by the formula:

\mu_{b}^{(4)}:{\bf e}_{1}\mapsto-{\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2}\mapsto-{\bf e}_{2},{\bf e}_{3}\mapsto{\bf e}_{3},{\bf e}_{4}\mapsto{\bf e}_{4}

5.0.3
Local coefficients and homology groups

Let us consider the homology group

H_{i}(K(({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}),1);{\mathbb{Z}})
.
In particular, for an odd
i
the group contains the fundamental classes of the manifolds

S^{i}/{\bf i}\times pt\subset S^{i}/{\bf i}\times S^{i}/{\bf i}\subset K({\bf J}_{b},1)\times K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1),

pt\times S^{i}/{\bf i}\subset S^{i}/{\bf i}\times S^{i}/{\bf i}\subset K({\bf J}_{b},1)\times K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1),

which are denoted by

\displaystyle t_{b,i},\in H_{i}(K({\bf J}_{b},1));\quad t_{{\dot{b}},i^{\prime}}\in H_{i^{\prime}}(K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1)).

(91)

A complete description of the group
H_{i}(K(({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}),1);{\mathbb{Z}})
is possible using the Kunneth formula, as in the case (
3
). A description of the standard base of the group

H_{i}(K({\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})
is described by:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}0\to\bigoplus_{i_{1}+i_{2}=i}H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf J}_{b},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow H_{i}(K({\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i_{1}+i_{2}=i-1}Tor^{{\mathbb{Z}}}(H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf J}_{b},1);{\mathbb{Z}}),H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\to 0.\\
\end{array}

Let us define the following local integer homology groups

\displaystyle H_{i}^{loc}(K(({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b})\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}^{(4)}}({\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}),1);{\mathbb{Z}}).

(96)

which was claimed as homology with interior system coefficients.
Local cycles in (
96
) are represented by oriented chains of the classifying space of the group (
76
) equipped with local paths to a marked point.
An analogous more simple system of interior local coefficients for the single generator was investigated in

[
A-P
]
.

Type I.
The local
{\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}
-coefficients system

\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}};I}^{(4)},\chi^{(4)}
acts along all the generators

\mu_{b}
,
\mu_{{\dot{b}}},\chi^{(4)}
by preserving orientation of local chains. In particular,
the generators of the local system acts (along paths) to global cycle as following: the generator

t_{b,i}
for
i\equiv 1\pmod{4}
is changed into the opposite along
the path
\mu_{b}
and is preserved for
i\equiv 1\pmod{4}
, the generator
t_{b,i}

is preserved along the path
\mu_{{\dot{b}}}
; the generators

t_{{\dot{b}},i^{\prime}}
for
i^{\prime}\equiv 1\pmod{4}
is changed into the opposite along
the path
\mu_{{\dot{b}}}
and is preserved for

i^{\prime}\equiv 3\pmod{4}
; the generator
t_{{\dot{b}},i^{\prime}}
along the path
\mu_{b}
is preserved. Because the homology group is described using generators
t_{b,i}
,
t_{{\dot{b}},i^{\prime}}
by (
5.0.3
), a global
action of the local system is well defined.

Type II.
The local
{\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}
-coefficients system

\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}};IIb}^{(4)},\chi^{(4)}

is determined by the same monodromy of cycles as Type I. The difference is the following: the orientation of chains is changed along the path
\mu_{b}

and along the path
\mu_{{\dot{b}}}
and is preserved along the simplest basic path of
\chi^{(4)}
.
The monodromy of the system is
described using generators as following:

t_{b,i}
,
i\equiv 1\pmod{4}
keeps the sign along
\mu_{b}

and changes the sign for
i\equiv 3\pmod{4}
along
\mu_{b}
, the generator keeps the sign along
\mu_{{\dot{b}}}
;
for the generators

t_{{\dot{b}},i^{\prime}}
the formula is analogous.

Let us determines classifying space for Type I local system. Consider a skeleton of the space

B({\bf J}_{b})\rtimes_{\mu_{b}}S^{1}\times B({\dot{\bf J}}_{b})\rtimes_{\mu_{{\dot{b}}}}S^{1}\rtimes_{\chi^{(4)}}S^{1}
, which is realized by means of the Laurent extension of the structured group

{\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}}\subset O(4+4)
, using (
5
),(
5.0.1
) of a bundle
over the corresponding Grasmann manifold

Gr_{{\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}(4+4,n)
of non-oriented
4+4=8
-plans in
n
–space.
Denote this Grasmann manifold (analogously to (
281
) by

\displaystyle KK({\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}})\subset K({\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}},1)\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}(S^{1}\times S^{1}).

(97)

On the space
(\ref{KK4})
a free involution

\displaystyle\chi^{[4]}:KK({\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}})\to KK({\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}}),

(98)

acts, this involution corresponds to the automorphism

(\ref{chi3A})
and represents by a permutation of planes of layers (by a block-antidiagonal matrix). The space for Type I system is constructed.

Let us determines classifying space for Type II local system. This is an analogous construction,
but, the extended Grasmann manifold

Gr_{{\bf J}_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}(4+4+2,n)
is required. The
2
-dimensional extension relates with the bundle (
87
). The space for Type II system is constructed.

Recall a general facts concerning constructed local coefficients system. Take a market point
pt
in (
97
) and take the image
\chi^{[4]}(pt)=pt^{t}
of this point by (
98
),
take a path
\lambda
, which joins

and
\chi^{[4]}(pt)
.
Using the path

a conjugated pair of local systems is defined:

\displaystyle\chi^{[4]}:\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}^{loc}\mapsto\mu_{{\dot{b}}\times b}^{loc}.

(99)

The left system in the pair admits the corresponding homology group (
96
),
the right system admits the conjugated homology group:

\displaystyle H_{i}^{loc}(K(({\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\times{\bf J}_{b})\rtimes_{\mu_{{\dot{b}}\times b}^{(4)}}({\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}}),1);{\mathbb{Z}}).

(100)

A pair of conjugated local systems (
100
) is well defined up to an equivalent relation.

The following lemma is analogous to Lemma

.

.

\bigoplus_{i_{1}+i_{2}=2s}H_{i_{1}}(K({\bf J}_{b},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes H_{i_{2}}(K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1);{\mathbb{Z}})\subset

Standardized

{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
-immersions

Let us formulate notions of standardized (and pre-standardized)

{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}
-immersion.

Let us consider a

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
of the codimension

8k
.
Assume that the image of the immersion
g
belongs to a regular neighborhood of an
embedding

I:S^{1}_{b}\times S^{1}_{{\dot{b}}}\rtimes_{\chi^{[4]}}S^{1}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

In this formula the third factor
S^{1}
is not a direct, the monodromy (an involution)
of a layer
S^{1}_{b}\times S^{1}_{{\dot{b}}}
along the exterior circle
is given by the permutation of the factors.
Denote by

U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
a regular thin neighborhood of the embedding
I
.

Let us consider
a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
–framed immersion

g:N^{n-8k}\looparrowright U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
for which the following condition (Y) of a control of the structured group of the normal bundle
is satisfied.

Condition (Y)

The immersion
g
admits a reduction of a symmetric structured group to the group
(
80
).
Additionally, the projection

\pi\circ g:N^{n-8k}\to S^{1}_{b}\times S^{1}_{{\dot{b}}}\rtimes_{\chi^{[4]}}S^{1}

of this immersion onto the central manifold of
U
is agreed with the projection
of the structured group

({\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}

onto the factors of the extension.

Formally, a weaker condition
is following.

Condition (Y1)

Assume an immersion
g
admits a reduction of a symmetric structured group to the group
(
80
).

Definition 16
.

Let us say that a

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
of the codimension

8k
is standardized if Condition Y is satisfied.

Let us say that

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
of the codimension

8k
is pre-standardized if the definition above week Condition (Y1) instead of Condition (Y) is formulated.
Classification problems of standardized
and of pre-standardized immersions are equivalent.

Standardized

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersions
generate a cobordism group, this group is naturally mapped into

Imm^{{\mathbb{Z}}/4^{[4]}}(n-8k,8k)
when a standardization is omitted.

The following definition is analogous to Definition

13
.
In this definition a projection of the Hurewich image of a fundamental class
onto a corresponding subgroup
is defined using Lemma

15
.

Definition 17
.

Let

(g,\eta_{N},\Psi)
be a standardized (a pre-standardized)

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion
in the codimension
8k
.
Let us say that this standardized immersion is pure, if the Hurewich image of a fundamental class
in

\in D_{n-8k}^{loc}({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}({\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}});{\mathbb{Z}}/2)

contains not monomial

t_{b,\frac{n-8k}{2}+i}\otimes t_{{\dot{b}},\frac{n-8k}{2}-i}
,
i\in\{\pm 1;\pm 2;\pm 3;\pm 4;\pm 5;\pm 6;\pm 7\}
, but, probably,
contains the only non-trivial monomial

.

.

Let us say that a

5.1
Dense Principle for formal self-intersected immersions

Let
f:M^{n-k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an immersion,
k\geq 1
. Let us consider the associated equivariant mapping
of Cartesian products:

f\times f:M\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, this mapping is also an immersion outside the diagonal in the origin.
The inverse image of the diagonal

(f\times f)^{-1}(\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
outside the diagonal
\mathrm{diag}_{M}
is a submanifold denoted by
\overline{\Delta}(f)

(previously the denotation
\overline{N}
was used).

Let us consider a sufficiently small

\varepsilon_{2}
neighbourhood
U_{\varepsilon_{2}}(\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
of the diagonal

\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
, such that the inverse image
(f\times f)^{-1}(\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
has no common points with

\overline{\Delta}(f)
. Then take a sufficiently small

\varepsilon_{1}
–neighbourhood
U_{\varepsilon_{1}}(\mathrm{diag}_{M})

of the diagonal
\mathrm{diag}_{M}
, such that
f\times f(U_{\varepsilon_{1}}(\mathrm{diag}_{M}))\subset U_{\varepsilon_{2}}(\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
.

Let us introduce the following denotation:

M^{(2)}=(M\times M)\setminus U_{\varepsilon_{1}}(\mathrm{diag}_{M}),

where the constant

\varepsilon_{1}
is defined explicitly by the construction.

Let us define a class of immersions, for which Dense Principle is formulated.

Definition 19
.

Let
M^{n-k}
be a closed manifold. Let us fix positive
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
. By
T:X\times X
(
X=M^{n-m},X={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
) the involution
T(x,y)=(y,x)
on the Cartesian product is denoted. Let us consider
T
-equivariant immersion
F:M^{(2)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Let us call such an immersion
pure immersion
, if

(1)
F(\partial M^{(2)})\subset U_{\varepsilon_{2}}(\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
;

(2)
F(\partial M^{(2)})\cap\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}=\emptyset
.

The both conditions can be refodmulated as a common condition:

F(\partial M^{(2)})\subset U_{\varepsilon_{2}}(\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\setminus\mathrm{diag}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

In the considered example the restriction

f\times f
on
M^{(2)}
is a pure immersion.

Proposition 20
.

Let
f_{0}:M\looparrowright R
be a smooth immersion of a compact closed manifold, assume that
R
is equipped with a metric
\operatorname{dist}
,
{\operatorname{dim}}(M)<{\operatorname{dim}}(R)
. Let
g:M\to R
be a continous mapping, which is homotopic to the immersion
f_{0}
(in our case
R={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and this extra assumption is unrequired). Then
\forall\varepsilon>0
there exists an immersion
f:M\looparrowright R
, which is regular homotopic to the immersion
f_{0}
, for which
\operatorname{dist}(g;f)_{C^{0}}<\varepsilon
in the metric induced by the metric
\operatorname{dist}
.

Доказательство.

This is a reformulation of
h
-principle by Hirsh
[
H
]
.
∎

Proposition 21
.

Let
(M^{(2)},\partial M^{(2)})
be a smooth manifold with a boundary, let

T_{M^{(2)}}:(M^{(2)},\partial M^{(2)})\to(M^{(2)},\partial M^{(2)})
be the transposition involution. Let
R^{(2)}
be a smooth manifold, which is equipped with the metric
dist
, and with the transposition involution
T_{R^{(2)}}:R^{(2)}\to R^{(2)}
, which admits a smooth submanifold of fixed points
\Delta_{R}\subset R^{(2)}
, and
{\operatorname{dim}}(R^{(2)})=2n
,
{\operatorname{dim}}(\Delta_{R})=n
,
{\operatorname{dim}}(M^{(2)})<{\operatorname{dim}}(R^{(2)})
.
We need the case
R^{(2)}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Assume that there exists a pure immersion (in the sence of Definition
19
), i.e.
T_{M},T_{R})
is an equivariant immersion

F_{0}^{(2)}:M^{(2)}\looparrowright R^{(2)}
,
and, additionally, the image of the boundary has no intersection with the fixed point manifold:

\displaystyle Im(F_{0}^{(2)}(\partial M^{(2)}))\subset R^{(2)}\setminus\Delta_{R}.

(101)

Let
G^{(2)}:M^{(2)}\to R^{(2)}
be a smooth
(T_{M^{(2)}},T_{R^{(2)}})
–equivariant mapping, for which the following condition is satisfied:

\displaystyle Im(G^{(2)}(\partial M^{(2)}))\subset R^{(2)}\setminus\Delta_{R}

(102)

and which is equivariant homotopic to an immersion

F_{0}^{(2)}
is the class of equivariant immersions described above. Then

\forall\varepsilon>0
there exists
(T_{M^{(2)}},T_{R^{(2)}})
–equivariant pure immersion (in the sence of definition described in Theorem
19
)

F_{1}^{(2)}:M^{(2)}\looparrowright R^{(2)}
, which is regular homotopic to an immersion

F_{0}^{(2)}
, for which the following conditions
\operatorname{dist}(F_{1}^{(2)};G^{(2)})_{C^{0}}<\varepsilon
in the metric, indused by the metric
\operatorname{dist}
,
R^{(2)}
.

Proof of Proposition
21

Let us consider a triangulation of the manifold

(M^{(2)},\partial M^{(2)})
of the caliber, much more smaller then
\varepsilon
. The induction over skeletons, analogously to Hirsh induction proves the proposition. ∎

Let us apply the Proposition
21
in the following situation. Let
(f,\varkappa,\Psi)
be a skew-framed immersion

f:M^{n-k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let us consider an open manifold
M^{n-k}\times M^{n-k}\setminus\Delta_{M}
, which is equipped with the involution
T_{M}
.
Denote by
M^{(2)}
a spherical blow-up of the manifold
M^{n-k}\times M^{n-k}\setminus\Delta_{M}
along the diagonal, equipped with the free involution

T^{(2)}_{M}
. (A spherical blow-up is homeomorphic to
M\times M
without a small regular delated
T^{(2)}_{M}
-equivariant neighborhood of the diagonal. The boundary of the manifold
M^{(2)}
is a fibred space of the spherical
S^{n-k-1}
-bundle over
M^{n-k}
. Let us denote by
\hat{M}^{(2)}

a quatient
M^{(2)}/T^{(2)}_{M}
. The boundary
\partial\hat{M}^{(2)}
coinsids with the projectivisation
TP(M^{n-k})
of the tangent bundle
T(M^{n-k})
. Let us denote
\hat{M}^{(2)}\setminus\partial\hat{M}^{(2)}
by
\hat{M}^{(2)}_{\circ}
.

Let
({\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{(2)}
be a manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, equipped with the involution
T^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
. The following mapping of configuration spaces

\displaystyle f^{(2)}:M^{(2)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(103)

which is a
(T^{(2)}_{M},T^{(2)}_{R})
–equivariant immersion is well defined. The equivariant immersion

(\ref{Gauss})
satisfies an analogous condition, as for
