in Proposition
21
.

Let us use a skew-framed framing

(\varkappa,\Psi)
for the immersion
f
to compute the normal bundle
\nu_{f^{(2)}}
of the immersion
f^{(2)}
.
Evidently, the immersion

\Psi
induces the isomorphism:

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}^{(2)}:\nu_{f^{(2)}}=k(\varkappa_{1}\oplus\varkappa_{2}),

(104)

where

\varkappa_{i}
is a linear bundle, assotiated with the immersion of the
i
-th factor
i=1,2
.

The involution

T^{(2)}_{M}
is covered by an involutive authomorphism of the bundle
\nu_{f^{(2)}}
(which is not the identity on the base), which permutes the corresponding lines in the right hand side of the formula
104
).
Therefore a corresponding vector byndle over
M^{(2)}
is well defined, let us denote this bundle by

\nu_{f^{(2)}}
. The isomorphism (
104
) induces the isomorphism

\displaystyle\Psi^{(2)}:\nu_{\hat{f}}^{(2)}=k(\eta),

(105)

where
\eta
is the 2-dimensional vector bundle with the structuring group
{\bf D}
.

Let us assume that the mapping (
103
) is transversal along the diagonal

\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and consider the inverce image
(f^{(2)})^{-1}(\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
of this diagonal, which is denoted by

\bar{N}\subset\bar{M}^{(2)}
. It is easy to check that
\bar{N}
is a closed manifold of the dimension
n-2k
.
The manifold
\bar{N}
is equipped with a free involution
T^{(2)}_{M}|_{\bar{N}}:\bar{N}\to\bar{N}
, the factormanifold is denoted by
N^{n-2k}
. The manifold
N^{n-2k}
is closed and is the formal analoug of the manifold, defined by the formula
(
21
).

This new definition is a more general, because this makes sence without assumption that the equivariant mapping

\bar{f}^{(2)}
is holonomic. This definition is possible for
(T^{(2)}_{M},T^{(2)}_{R})
–equivariant mappings, which satisfies the condition (
101
), from a regular equivariant homotopy class of the mapping
f^{(2)}
.
The immersion
\bar{N}^{n-2k}\looparrowright M^{n-k}
and its restriction
f|_{\bar{N}^{n-2k}}:\bar{N}^{n-2k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
are well defined.
The normal bundle of the mapping
f|_{\bar{N}^{n-2k}}
is isomorphic to the restriction of the bundle
\nu_{f^{(2)}}
on the submanifold

\bar{N}\subset M^{(2)}
.

Let us consider the mapping

g:N^{n-2k}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, which is defined as a factormapping of the restriction of
f^{(2)}
on
\bar{N}
.
From the transversality condition for

f^{(2)}
along the diagonal we get that
g
is a local embedding, i.e. an immersion.
The normal bundle

\nu_{g}
of the immersion
g
is naturally isomorphic to the restriction of the bundle
\nu_{f}^{(2)}
on the submanifold

N^{n-2k}\subset M^{(2)}
. The dihedral framing, which is defined by the formula (
105
), coinsids with a dihedral framing
\Psi
.

Assume that an arbitrary

(T^{(2)}_{M},T^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
–equivariant immersion

G^{(2)}:M^{(2)}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
which satisfies the condition
(\ref{uslG})
, and is equivariant homotopic to the immersion

(\ref{Gauss})
with a prescribed condition is well defined. In particular, the isomorphism
(\ref{D})
is preserved by a regular homotopy. Assume that a regular homotopy keeps the condition
(\ref{uslG})
and is transwersal along

\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let us consider the manifold
N^{n-2k}(G)
in the target. This manifold is closed, and an immersion
g(G):N^{n-2k}(G)\looparrowright\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is well defined as the restriction of
G
.
The normal bundle of this immersion, which is denoted by
\nu_{g(G)}
, is isomorphic to the restriuction of the nornmal bundle of the immersion

G/T_{M}
. The normal bundle
\nu_{g(G)}
is equipped with a dihedral framing, which is defined by the formula
(\ref{D})
.
Therefore the following

{\bf D}
–framed immersuion
(g(G),\eta(G),\Psi(G))
, which is regular homotopic to the
{\bf D}
–framed immersion

(g,\eta,\Psi)
of the self-intersection manifold of the origine skew-framed immersion
(f,\varkappa,\Xi)
is well defined. The constructed
{\bf D}
-framed immersion

(g,\eta,\Psi)
satisfies additional properties, which are defined explicitely strating from the immersion

(\ref{Gauss})
.

5.2
Dense Principle for formal iterated self-intersected immersions

Formal regular homotopy

Let
g:N\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an arbitrary immersion.
This immersion induces a
(T_{N},T_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
- equivariant mapping
g^{(2)}:N^{(2)}=N\times N\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, where
T_{N}
is a permutation of points in the origin,
T_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is a permutation in the target. We will call that

F_{t}^{(2)}:N\times N\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a formal generic regular deformation of
g^{(2)}

F_{0}^{(2)}=g^{(2)}
and the following conditions are satisfied:

–1. a support
supp(F^{(2)})\subset N\times N
of
F^{(2)}
is outside the diagonal
\Delta_{N}\subset N\times N
;

–2.
F^{(2)}
is a regular
(T_{N},T_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
-equivariant homotopy on
supp(F)
;

–3.
F^{(2)}
is regular along
\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
.

For an arbitrary formal regular homotopy of an immersion
g
to a formal immersion
g_{1}^{(2)}
the self-intersection manifold
L
,
h:L\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, of
g
is regular cobordant to the manifold
L_{1}
of formal self-intersection of
g^{(2)}_{1}
, which is defined by

(g^{(2)}_{1})^{-1}(\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})/T_{N}
. For generic
F^{(2)}
the manifold
L_{1}
is equipped with a parametrized immersion
h_{1}:L_{1}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and the immersions
h
and
h_{1}
of manifolds
L
and
L_{1}

are regular cobordant. In the case
g
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s]}
-framed immersion,
the immersion
h
is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s+1]}
-framed and
L_{1}
and the cobordism between
L
and
L_{1}
are also
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s+1]}
-framed. This statement is a straghtforward generalisation of the construction
for skew-framed immersion, described in
5.1
to the case of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s]}
-framed immersions.

We need a generalisation of the construction described in Subsection
5.1
and we will consider a formal self-intersection immersed manifold of a formal self-intersection immersed manifold of an original immersion
g
in a common construction.

The immersion
g
induces a
(T^{(2)}_{N},T^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
- equivariant mapping
g^{(2+2)}:(N\times N)\times(N\times N)\looparrowright({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\times({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, where
T^{(2)}_{N}
is the involution of involutions by permutation of pairs (in a common bracket) of points in the origin,
T^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
is a permutation of pairs of points in the target.
The restriction
g^{(2+2)}
on a formal self-intersection manofold of
g^{(2)}
is an immersion and we may apply the iteration of the definition to consider a formal self-intersection points of this restriction.

We will call that

F_{t}^{(2+2)}:(N\times N)\times(N\times N)\looparrowright({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\times({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})

is a formal generic regular deformation of
g^{(2+2)}
,

F_{0}^{(2+2)}=g^{(2+2)}
if the following conditions are satisfied:

–1. a support
supp(F^{(2+2)})\subset(N\times N)\times(N\times N)
of
F^{(2+2)}
is outside the thick diagonal
\Delta_{N}^{(2)}\subset(N\times N)\times(N\times N)
;

–2.
F^{(2+2)}
is a regular
(T^{(2)}_{N},T^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
-equivariant homotopy on
supp(F)
;

–3.
F^{(2+2)}
is regular along
\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{(2)}\subset({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\times({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
.

An arbitrary generic immersion
g:N\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with a self-intersection manifold
h:L\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
admits an iterated self-intersection manifold

f:M\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

which is a self-intersection manifold of
h
.
For an arbitrary formal regular homotopy
F^{(2+2)}
of the extension
g^{(2+2)}

g
to a formal immersion
g_{1}^{(2+2)}
the iterated self-intersection manifold
M
,
f:M\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, of
g
is regular cobordant to the manifold
M_{1}
of formal iterated self-intersection of
g^{(2+2)}_{1}
, which is defined by

(g^{(2+2)}_{1})^{-1}(\Delta^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})/T^{(2)}_{N}
. For generic
F^{(2+2)}
the manifold
M_{1}
is equipped with a parametrized immersion
f_{1}:M_{1}\looparrowright{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and the immersions
f
and
f_{1}
of manifolds
M
and
M_{1}

are regular cobordant. In the case
g
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s]}
-framed immersion,
the immersion
f
is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s+2]}
-framed and
M_{1}
and the cobordism between
M
and
M_{1}
are also
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[s+2]}
-framed.

The definition of formal homotopies (with no regular condition) should be applied for generic singular mappings.

Theorem 22
.

Let

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
be a standardized pure
{\bf D}
-framed immersion
in the codimension
2k
(see Definitions
3
,
13
),

k\geq 7
. Let

(h,\Psi_{L},\eta_{L})
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[3]}
-framed immersion of self-intersection points of
g
;

(f,\Psi_{M},\zeta_{M})
be the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-framed immersion
of iterated self-intersection points of
g
.

Then in each regular homotopy class of
{\bf D}
-framed standardized immersions there exist
(g,\Psi,\eta)
and a formal regular deformation
g^{(2+2)}\mapsto\bar{g}^{(2+2)}
, the iterated self-intersection manifold of
\bar{g}^{(2+2)}

is a disjoin union of a pre-standardized pure
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-immersion

(f,\Xi_{M},\eta_{M})
(see Condition (Y1)) and a negligible
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-immersion.

Moreover, the cobordism class of the of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
-standardized immersion

(f,\Xi_{M},\eta_{M})
is well defined for

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
in its regular cobordism class.

A sketch of a proof of Theorem
22

In Subsubsection
5.3.1
a spesial (non-generic)
PL
-mapping
d^{(2)}
, which is a ramified
{\mathbb{Z}}/4\times{\mathbb{Z}}/4
covering, is constructed. A formal self-intersections of
d^{(2)}
is a polyhedron, which is explisitely described in Section
8
. This polyhedron admits
a subpolyhedron (
118
), which becomes a closed component after a vertical formal deformation of the mapping
d^{(2)}
, described in Lemma
25
. This closed component is a support of a characteristic class, the cohomology class, which is detected Arf-invariant, as is proved in Lemma
49
. The proof itself of Theorem
22
is presented at the last part of the section, estimations of the regular codimension for
n=126
are restricted, in the case
n=254,\dots
calculations are not restricted. Also for
n=254,\dots
an alternative approach using a straghforvard iteration of the Dence Principle, described in Subsection
5.1
gives simplifications, because the most Section
8
is unnesesary.

5.3
Proof of Theorem
22

5.3.1
Construction of the mapping
d^{(2)}

Consider the standard covering
p:S^{1}\to S^{1}
of the degree
4
.
It is convenient to write-down:

p:S^{1}\to S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}
,
by the quotient:

{\mathbb{Z}}/4\times S^{1}\to S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}
.

Consider the join
of
\frac{n^{\prime}-k+1}{2}=r
-copies of the circle

S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}

S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}\ast\dots\ast S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}=S^{n^{\prime}-k}
,
which is PL-homeomorphic to the standard

n^{\prime}-k
-dimensional sphere. Let us define the join of
r
copies of the mapping

p
:

\tilde{d}:S^{1}\ast\dots\ast S^{1}\to S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}\ast\dots\ast S^{1}/\{{\bf i}\}.

On the pre-image acts the group

{\mathbb{Z}}/4
by the diagonal action, this action is commuted with

\tilde{d}
. The mapping
\bar{d}
is defined by the composition of the quotient

\tilde{d}/\{{\bf i}\}:S^{n^{\prime}-k}/\{{\bf i}\}\to S^{n^{\prime}-k}
with the standard inclusion

S^{n^{\prime}-k}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{\prime}}.

A formal (non-holonomic, vertical) small deformation of the formal (holonomic) extension of the mapping
\bar{d}
is the required mapping

d^{(2)}
.

5.3.2
Construction of the mapping
d^{(2+2)}

The mapping

\bar{d}:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n^{\prime}-k}\to S^{n^{\prime}-k}

has to be generalized, using two-stages tower
(
109
) of ramified coverings.

Let us recall, that a positive integer
m_{\sigma}=14
.
Denote by

ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

the Cartesian product o standard lens space
\pmod{4}
, namely,

\displaystyle ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}=S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}/{\bf i}\times S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}/{\bf i}.

(106)

Evidently,
{\operatorname{dim}}(ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}})=\frac{7}{4}(n+m_{\sigma})+2>n
.

On the space
ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
a free involution
\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}:ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}\to ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
acts by the formula:

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}(x\times y)=(y\times x)
.

Let us define a subpolyhedron (a manifold with singularities)

X_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}\subset ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
.
Let us consider the following family

\{X_{j},\quad j=0,1,\dots,j_{max}\}
,
j_{max}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
, of submanifolds
ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
:

X_{0}=S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}/{\bf i}\times S^{1}/{\bf i},

X_{1}=S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}-1}/{\bf i}\times S^{3}/{\bf i},\quad\dots

X_{j}=S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1-2j}/{\bf i}\times S^{2j+1}/{\bf i},\quad

X_{j_{max}}=S^{1}/{\bf i}\times S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}/{\bf i},

where

\displaystyle j_{max}=\frac{7n+m_{\sigma}}{16}=2^{n-1},\quad m_{\sigma}=14.

(107)

The dimension of each manifold in this family equals to

n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+2
and the codimension in
ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
equals to

n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}
. Let us define
an embedding

X_{j}\subset ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

by a Cartesian product of the two standard inclusions.

Let us denote by

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}:ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}\to ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

the involution, which permutes coordinates.
Evidently, we get:

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}(X_{j})=X_{j_{max}-j}
.

A polyhedron

X_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{j_{max}}X_{j}\subset ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

is well defined. This polyhedron is invariant with respect to the
involution

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
.
The polyhedron

X_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

can be considered as a stratified manifolds with strata of the codimension 2.

The restriction of the involution

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

on the polyhedron

X_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
denote by

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}
.

Write-down the sequence of the subgroups of the index
2
of the diagram

(\ref{a,aa})
:

\displaystyle{\bf I}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf I}}_{a}\longrightarrow{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}\longrightarrow{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}.

(108)

Define the following tower of
2
-sheeted coverings, which is associated with the sequence

(\ref{zep})
:

\displaystyle ZZ_{a\times\dot{a}}\longrightarrow ZZ_{{\bf H}_{a}\times\dot{a}}\longrightarrow ZZ_{{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}.

(109)

The bottom space of the tower

(\ref{z})
coincides to a skeleton of the Eilenberg-MacLane space:

ZZ_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}\subset K({\bf J}_{a},1)\times K({\dot{\bf J}}_{a},1)
.
This tower (
110
) determines the tower
(\ref{zz})
by means of the inclusion

ZZ_{{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}\subset K({\bf J}_{b},1)\times K({\dot{\bf J}}_{b},1)
of the bottom.

Let us define the following tower of double coverings:

\displaystyle X_{a\times\dot{a}}\longrightarrow X_{{\bf H}_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}\longrightarrow X_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}.

(110)

The bottom space of the tower

(\ref{z})

is a subspace of the bottom space of the tower

(\ref{zz})
by means of an inclusion

X_{{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}\subset ZZ_{{\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}}
.
The tower

(\ref{z})

determines as the restriction of the tower

(\ref{zz})

on this subspace.

Let us describe a polyhedron

X_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset ZZ_{a\times\dot{a}}

explicitly. Let us define a family

\{X^{\prime}_{0},X^{\prime}_{1},\dots,X^{\prime}_{j_{max}}\}

of standard submanifolds in the manifold

ZZ_{a\times\dot{a}}={\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}

by the following formulas:

\displaystyle X^{\prime}_{0}={\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{1}\dots

(111)

X^{\prime}_{j}={\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1-2j}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2j+1}\dots

X^{\prime}_{j_{max}}={\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{1}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}.

In this formulas the integer index

j_{max}

is defined by the formula

(\ref{jmax})
.
The polyhedron

X_{a\times\dot{a}}\subset ZZ_{a\times\dot{a}}

is defined
as the union of standard submanifolds in this family.
The polyhedron

X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}\subset ZZ_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}

a quotient of the double covering, which corresponds to the tower of the groups.

The spaces

X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}
,
X_{a\times\dot{a}}

admit free involutions, which are pullbacks of the involution

\chi_{{\bf J}_{a}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{a}}

by the projection on the bottom space of the tower.

The cylinder of the involution

\chi_{a\times\dot{a}}
is well defined,
(correspondingly, of the involution
\chi_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}
), which is denoted by

X_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}S^{1}

(correspondingly, by

X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}S^{1}
).
The each space is embedded into the corresponding fibred space over

{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
:

X_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}S^{1}\subset X_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2},

X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}S^{1}\subset X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}.

Then let us define a polyhedron

J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}
,
which is a base of a ramified covering

X_{a\times\dot{a}}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}
.

Then let us extend the ramified covering over the bottom space of the tower to the ramified covering:

X_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
,
and the ramified covering over the middle space of the tower of the ramified covering

X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
.

Let us define a polyhedron (a manifold with singularities)

J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}
.
For an arbitrary

j=0,1,\dots,j_{max}
, where
j_{max}

is defined
by the formula

(\ref{jmax})
, let us define the polyhedron

J_{j}=S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}-2j+1}\times S^{2j+1}

(the Cartesian product).
Spheres (components of this Cartesian product)

S^{n-\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}-2j+1}
,

are re-denoted by

J_{j,1}
,
J_{j,2}

correspondingly.
Using this denotations, we get:

J_{j}=J_{j,1}\times J_{j,2}.

The standard inclusion

i_{j}:J_{j,1}\times J_{j,2}\subset S^{\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}\times S^{\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}

is well defined, each factor is included into the target sphere as the standard subsphere.

The union

\bigcup_{j=0}^{j_{max}}Im(i_{j})

of images of this embeddings is denoted by

\displaystyle J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}\subset S^{\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}\times S^{\frac{n-m_{\sigma}}{8}+1}.

(112)

The polyhedron

J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}
is constructed.

Let us define a ramified covering

\displaystyle\varphi_{a\times\dot{a}}:X_{a\times\dot{a}}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}.

(113)

The covering
(
113
)
is defined as the union of the Cartesian products of the ramified coverings,
which was constructed in Subsubsection

5.3.1
.

The covering
(
113
)
is factorized into the following ramified covering:

\displaystyle\varphi_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}:X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}.

(114)

Because

X_{a\times\dot{a}}\to X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}

is a double covering, the number of sheets of the covering
(

) is greater by the factor

,
where

is the denominator of the ramification.

The polyhedron

J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}

is equipped by the involution

\chi
, which is defined analogously to the involutions

\chi_{a\times\dot{a}}
,
\chi_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}
.

The cylinder of the involution is well defined,
let us denote this cylinder by

J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}S^{1}
.
The inclusion

J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}S^{1}\subset J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}
.
is well defined.

The ramified covering

(\ref{varphiX})
commutes with the involutions

\chi_{a\times\dot{a}}
,
\chi_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}

in the origin and the target.

Therefore the ramified covering

\displaystyle c_{X}:X_{a\times\dot{a}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2},

(115)

which is factorized into the ramified covering

\displaystyle c_{Y}:X_{{\bf H}_{a\times\dot{a}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\to J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}.

(116)

is well defined.

Lemma 23
.

There exist an inclusion

\displaystyle i:J_{b\times{\dot{b}}}{\rtimes}_{\chi}{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{2}\times D^{5}\rtimes D^{3}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

,

.

is neglected (the Hurewicz image of the fundamental class (which exists)

3
.

.

Proof of Lemma
26

Additionally, one has to consider, as this automorphism is defined on

-dimensional quaternionic lens

(the quaternionic action on

is on the right).

The automorphism is given by the transformation of

, which is the right multiplication of the quaternion unite.
The automorphism

is commuted with the transformation of
the quaternion units (the only non-obvious calculation concerns the unite

\bf{k}
). Therefore on the quotient

S^{3}/{\bf Q}
the automorphism is well defined.

The trivialization of the tangent bundle on the quaternionic lens, which is defined by the left action of
the units
({\bf i},{\bf j},\bf{k})
,
is changed by corresponding automorphisms. Therefore the image of the quaternion framing by the differential

\displaystyle d(\mu_{{\bf Q}}):T(S^{3}/{\bf Q})\to T(S^{3}/{\bf Q})

(146)

is fibred isotopic to the identity. This isotopy consists of a family of rotations
trough the angle

in oriented planes, which are orthogonal to the vectors

\bf{k}
.

(
136
) is extend to the homomorphism of the Laurent extension from the subgroup

of the index
2
to the all group:

\displaystyle({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2)\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}^{(5)}}{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}{\rtimes}_{\chi^{[5]}}{\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[5]}.

(147)

In the following Lemma the main result is proved.

Theorem 30
.

Let
(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})

{\mathbb{Z}}/2^{[4]}
be a standardized (see Definition
16
)
immersion, with
{\bf Q}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/4
-structure (see Definition
27
).
Let us assume that
(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
is pure (see Definition
17
).
Then

(g,\Psi,\eta_{N})
is negligible.

Proof of Theorem
30

We use a non-standard modification of the Herbert formula with local

\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}},\chi^{(4)}
coefficients valued in the group described in Lemma
15
, and for immersions with additional marked component, which gives an extra contribution on the both sides of the Herbert formula.
The Euler class of the bundle
(
87
) is an immersed submanifold in the codimension 2, possibly, with double self-intersections. Denote this submanifold by
N_{1}^{n-8k-2}\subset N^{n-8k}
.

Let us restrict the immersion
g
on the submanifold

N_{1}^{n-8k-2}\looparrowright N
and let us consider the manifold
L_{1}^{n_{\sigma}-4}\looparrowright L^{n_{\sigma}}

of self-intersection points of the immersed manifold

g(N_{1}^{n-8k-2})
. We use arguments related with the Herbert immersion theorem
[
He
]
.

The characteristic number, the image of the fundamental class

[\bar{L}_{1}]
by the characteristic mapping
D_{n_{\sigma}-4}^{loc}({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}({\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}});{\mathbb{Z}})
,
is calculated using the Euler classes of the structured
4
-bundle over
L
and
the image
[\bar{L}^{n_{\sigma}}]
, which is an element in

D_{n_{\sigma}}^{loc}({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}({\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}});{\mathbb{Z}})
.
This calculation of also possible by the Herbert theorem, which is applied to
[\bar{L}]
. The self-intersection points of the immersion
N_{1}^{n-8k-2}\looparrowright N^{n-8k}
give an extra contribution to self-intersection points of
g|_{N_{1}}
. In the Herbert
formula for
[\bar{L}_{1}^{n_{\sigma}-4}]
this extra classes
are cancelled in the both sides in the formula.

The characteristic classes takes values modulo 4. By arguments as in
[
A-P
]
, this characteristic class in
D_{n_{\sigma-4}}^{loc}({\bf J}_{b}\times{\dot{\bf J}}_{b}\rtimes_{\mu_{b\times{\dot{b}}}}({\mathbb{Z}}\times\dot{{\mathbb{Z}}});{\mathbb{Z}})

is opposite to itself. Because
g
is pure, this proves that the characteristic number is even.
Theorem
30
is proved. ∎

7

In this section we assume that

;

7.1.1
Axillary mappings
c:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

7.1.2
Deleted square

Let us consider the following space

\displaystyle({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}\setminus\Delta)/T^{\prime}=\Gamma_{\circ},

The subspace

This space (
167
) is defined by the factor on the Cartesian product by the involution

, which permutes the coordinates, outside the diagonal.
The deleted product is an open manifold.

7.1.3
The mapping
c_{0}
(

),

(

7.1.4
The structuring mappings
\eta_{{\bf N}_{\circ}}:{\bf N}_{\circ}\to K({\bf D},1)
for the mappings
c_{0}
,
c

Let us define the mapping

\displaystyle\eta_{\Gamma_{\circ}}:\Gamma_{\circ}\to K({\bf D},1),

(170)

which is called the structuring mapping.

The inclusion

\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}
induces an isomorphism of the fundamental groups, because the codimension of the diagonal
\Delta\subset{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}
equals

and satisfies the following inequality:

. Therefore, the following equation is satisfied:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ})=H_{1}(\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)={\mathbb{Z}}/2\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2.

(171)

Let us consider the induced automorphism

T^{\prime}_{\ast}:H_{1}(\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)\to H_{1}(\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ};{\mathbb{Z}}/2)
, for the definition of

see (
167
). This automorphism is not the identity. Let us fix the standard isomorphism

with
{\bf I}_{A\times\dot{A}}
, this isomorphism maps the generator of the first factor (correspondingly, the generator of the second factor) of the group

(see
(
171

(correspondingly, to the element

),
which is represented in the representation of

{\bf D}
by the symmetry with respect to the second (correspondingly, with respect to the first) coordinate axis.

It is easy to check that the automorphism of external conjugation of the subgroup

{\bf I}_{A\times\dot{A}}\subset{\bf D}
on the element
b\in{\bf D}\setminus{\bf I}_{A\times\dot{A}}
(in this formula the element

b\in{\bf I}_{b}
is a generator), which is defined by the formula:

, is transformed by the considered isomorphism into the automorphism
T^{\prime}_{\ast}
. The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Gamma_{\circ})

is a quadratic extension of the group
\pi_{1}(\bar{\Gamma})

by the element
b
, this extension is uniquely determined (up to an equivalence) by the automorphism
T^{\prime}_{\ast}
. Therefore

and the mapping

\eta_{\Gamma_{\circ}}:\Gamma_{\circ}\to K({\bf D},1)
is well defined.

The structuring mapping

is defined by the restriction of

on the open subpolyhedron (
169
). The construction depends of

(

),

(

).

7.2.1
The stratification of the sphere
J

Let us ordered 1-dimensional lens spaces (circles), which are generators of the join, by positive integers

1,\dots,r
, and denote by
J(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})\subset J

the subjoin, which is defined by the join of the subcollection of
circles

S^{1}/{\bf i}
with the numbers
1\leq k_{1}<\dots<k_{s}\leq r
,
0\geq s\geq r
.
The stratification is induced by the stratification of the standard
r
-dimensional simplex

\delta^{r}
by the projection
J\to\delta^{r}
.
The inverse images of the vertexes of the simplex are circles

J(j)\subset J
,
J(j)\approx S^{1}
,
1\leq j\leq r
, which are irreducible components of the join.
The circle looks like the base
S^{1}/-1
of
2
-sheeted covering for the mapping (
166
), and like the base
S^{1}/{\bf i}
for
4
-sheeted covering for the mapping (
165
).

Let us define the space

J_{1}^{[s]}
as a subspace in
J
as the union of all subspaces
J(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})\subset J
.

Therefore, the following stratification

\displaystyle J^{(r)}\subset\dots\subset J^{(1)}\subset J^{(0)},

(172)

of the space
J
is well defined.
For a given stratum the number

r-s
of omitted coordinates is called the deep of the stratum.

Let us introduce the following denotations:

\displaystyle J^{[i]}=J^{(i)}\setminus J^{(i+1)}.

(173)

7.2.2
The stratification of
{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k}

Let us define the stratification of the projective space

{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}
.Denote the maximal open cell

p^{-1}(J(k_{1},\dots,k_{s}))
by
U(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})\subset S^{n-k+1}/-1
.
This cell is called an elementary stratum of the deep
r-s
.
A point on an elementary stratum

is defined by the collection of coordinates

(\check{x}_{k_{1}},\dots,\check{x}_{k_{s}},l)
, where
\check{x}_{k_{i}}
is the coordinate
on the circle, which is covered the circle of the join with the number

,

is a coordinate on

(s-1)
-dimensional simplex of the join.
Points on an elementary stratum

U(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
belong to the union of subcomplexes of the join
with corresponding coordinates.

7.2.3
The stratification of the polyhedron

(

) for the mapping

(

Let us start with the case

The polyhedron

Let us denote tis strata by

\displaystyle K^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq r.

(174)

Let us consider exceptional antidiagonal strata of

inside the anti-diagonal

\displaystyle\Delta_{anti}=\{([x,y])\in\Gamma_{0}:x={\bf i}y\}.

(175)

An open polyhedron, which is defined by the cutting-out of anti-diagonal strata from

is denoted by

.

Let us describe an elementary stratum

For the simplicity of denotations, let us consider the case

Assume a pair of points

({\bf x}_{1}
,
{\bf x}_{2})
determines a point on

the collection of spherical coordinates of each point.
A spherical coordinate determines a point on the circle
with the same number

, the circle is a covering over the corresponding coordinate lens

J(i)\subset J
in the join. Recall, that the pair of coordinates
with a common index determines a par in a layer of the standard cyclic

{\bf i}
-covering
S^{1}\to S^{1}/{\bf i}
.

Collections of coordinates
\{(\check{x}_{1,i},\check{x}_{2,i})\}

are considered up to a transformation of the full collection into the antipodal collection. Moreover, a non-ordered pair of points

[{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}]
is lifted into a pair of points
(\bar{\bf x}_{1},\bar{\bf x}_{2})
on the sphere
S^{n-k}
not uniquely.
The collection of coordinates of a lift is defined up to

8
different transformations. (Up to transformations of the group
{\bf D}
of the order
8
.)

Analogous statement is well-formulated for points in deeper elementary strata

K^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
,
1\leq s\leq r
.

7.2.4
The stratification of the polyhedron

{\bf N}_{\circ}
(
169
) for the mapping
c
(
166
)

The polyhedron

{\bf N}_{\circ}
, (
169
) is the disjoin union
of elementary strata, which are defined as inverse images of the strata

(\ref{strat[J]})
analogously with the case in the subsection
7.2.3
.
Let us denote this strata by (
8.2.3
) as in the construction for the mapping
c_{0}
.
In the considered case exceptional antidiagonal strata on
{\bf N}_{\circ}
are absent.

Let us describe an elementary stratum

K^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
by means of coordinate system.
Assume a pair of points

({\bf x}_{1}
,
{\bf x}_{2})
determines a point on

K^{[0]}(1,\dots,r)
, let us fixes for the pair a pair of points

(\check{\bf x}_{1},\check{\bf x}_{2})
on the covering sphere

S^{n-k}
, which is mapped into the pair
({\bf x}_{1}
,

{\bf x}_{2})
by the projection
S^{n-k+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k+1}
.
Denote by

(\check{\bf x}_{1,i},\check{\bf x}_{2,i})
,
i=1,\dots,r

the collection of spherical coordinates of each point.
Recall, that the pair of coordinates
with a common index determines a pair in a layer of the standard
2
-fold covering
S^{1}\to S^{1}/-1
.

Collections of coordinates
(\check{x}_{1,i},\check{x}_{2,i})

are considered up to antipodal transformations of the full collection into the antipodal collection. Moreover, pairs of points

({\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2})
are non-ordered and lifts of a point in
K
to a pair of points
(\bar{\bf x}_{1},\bar{\bf x}_{2})
on the sphere
S^{n-k+1}
are not uniquely defined.
Therefore collection of coordinates are defined up to

8
different transformations. (Up to transformation of the group
{\bf D}
of the order
8
.)

Analogous statement is well-formulated for points in deeper elementary strata

K^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
,
1\leq s\leq r
.

7.2.5
Coordinates on

K_{\circ}\subset{\bf N}_{\circ}
, the mapping
c_{0}
(
165
)

Let

x\in K^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
be a point on an elementary stratum.
Let us consider collections of spherical coordinates

\check{x}_{1,i}
,
\check{x}_{2,i}
,
k_{1}\leq i\leq k_{s}
,
1\leq i\leq r_{0}

of the point
x
. For each
i
the following cases are possible:
the pairs of
i
-th coordinates coincides; antipodal; the second coordinate is the result of the transformation of the first coordinate by the generator
{\bf i}
of the cyclic cover
S^{1}\mapsto S^{1}/{\bf i}
.
Let us define for an ordered pair of coordinates

\check{x}_{1,i}
,
\check{x}_{2,i}
,
1\leq i\leq r
a residue

v_{i}
with values
+1
,
-1
,
+{\bf i}
,
-{\bf i}
:

v_{k_{i}}=\check{x}_{1,k_{i}}(\check{x}_{2,k_{i}})^{-1}
.

By transformation of the collection of coordinates

\check{x}_{2}
into the antipodal collection, the collection of residues
are transformed by the antipodal opposition:

\{(\check{x}_{1,k_{i}},\check{x}_{2,k_{i}})\}\mapsto\{(-\check{x}_{1,k_{i}},\check{x}_{2,k_{i}})\},\quad\{v_{k_{i}}\}\mapsto\{-v_{k_{i}}\},

\{(\check{x}_{1,k_{i}},\check{x}_{2,k_{i}})\}\mapsto\{(\check{x}_{1,k_{i}},-\check{x}_{2,k_{i}})\},\quad\{v_{k_{i}}\}\mapsto\{-v_{k_{i}}\}.

By transformation of the collections of coordinates by the renumeration:

(\check{x}_{1},\check{x}_{2})\mapsto(\check{x}_{2},\check{x}_{1})
,
the collection of residues are transformed by the complex conjugated collection:
into the antipodal collection, the collection of residues
are transformed by the antipodal opposition:

\{(\check{x}_{1,k_{i}},\check{x}_{2,k_{i}})\}\mapsto\{(\check{x}_{2,k_{i}},\check{x}_{1,k_{i}})\},\quad\{v_{k_{i}}\}\mapsto\{\bar{v}_{k_{i}}\},

where
v\mapsto\bar{v}
means the complex conjugation.
Evidently, the collection of residues remains fixed in the case a different point on the elementary stratum is marked.

Elementary strata
K^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
,
accordingly with collections of coordinates, are divided into 3 types:

{\bf I}_{b},{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},{\bf I}_{d}
. In the case residues takes values in

\{+{\bf i},-{\bf i}\}
(correspondingly, in
\{+1,-1\}
), one say that an elementary stratum is of the type

{\bf I}_{b}
(correspondingly, of the type
{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
).
In the case there are residues of the complex type

\{+{\bf i},-{\bf i}\}
, and the real type
\{+1,-1\}
simultaneously, one shall say
about an elementary stratum of the type
{\bf I}_{d}
.
It is easy to check that the restriction of the characteristic mapping

\eta:K_{\circ}\to K({\bf D},1)

on the elementary stratum of the type
{\bf I}_{b},{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},{\bf I}_{d}
correspondingly, is a composition of a mapping into the space
K({\bf I}_{b},1)

(correspondingly, into the space
{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
, or
K({\bf I}_{d},1)
) with
the mapping
i_{b}:K({\bf I}_{b},1)\to K({\bf D},1)
(correspondingly with the mapping
i_{a\times\dot{a}}:K({\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}},1)\to K({\bf D},1)
, or with
i_{d}:K({\bf I}_{d},1)\to K({\bf D},1)
).
For strata of the first two types a reduction
of the structuring mapping (up to homotopy) is not well defined; this reduction
is well defined only up to the conjugation
of the subgroup

{\bf I}_{b}
(correspondingly, in the subgroup
{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}
).

Recall, the polyhedron

{\bf N}_{\circ}
is defined by addition elementary anti-diagonal strata to the polyhedron
K_{\circ}
.
On each elementary anti-diagonal stratum

K(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
a residue of each coordinate equals
+{\bf i}
.

Define the following open polyhedrons:

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{b}\circ}\subset K_{\circ}\subset{\bf N}_{\circ}

(176)

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}}\circ}\subset K_{\circ}\subset{\bf N}_{\circ}

(177)

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{d}\circ}\subset K_{\circ}\subset{\bf N}_{\circ}

(178)

as polyhedrons, which are obtain as union of all elementary strata of the corresponding type.
The anti-diagonal stratum (formally) is a stratum of the type

{\bf I}_{b}
, with this convenient the inclusion (
176
) is modified into

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{b}}\mapsto K_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}\cup\Delta_{anti}\subset{\bf N}_{\circ}.

(179)

7.2.7
A prescribed coordinate system on an elementary stratum

Let us recall that the polyhedron

K_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}
is decomposed into a disjoint union of elementary strata

K(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
,
0\leq s\leq r
, residues of angles coordinates take values
\{+{\bf i},-{\bf i}\}
.
On each elementary stratum with odd (even) angles

\alpha
of the type
{\bf I}_{{\bf b}}
let us define a coordinate system, which is called a prescribed coordinate system
\Omega(\alpha)
.

Assume that
s
is odd. Let us call a coordinate system a prescribed system, if
a number of coordinates with the residues
+{\bf i}
is greater then the number of coordinates with the residues
-{\bf i}
.
In the case the residues are divided in the middle, let us define the prescribed coordinate
system by the following convinient: the residue with the smallest number equals to
+1
.

An analogous definition for the polyhedron
K_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}
is presented.
Let us call a coordinate system a prescribed system, if
a number of coordinates with the residues
+1
is greater then the number of coordinates with the residues
-1
.
In the case the residues are divided in the middle (this is possible only if
s
is even), let us define the prescribed coordinate system such that the residues with the smallest index equals to
+1
.

7.2.8
Admissible boundary strata of elementary strata

For an arbitrary elementary stratum

\beta\subset\hat{K}^{[r-s,i]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
of the type

{\bf I}_{{\bf b}}
(correspondingly, of the type
{\bf I}_{\bf a\times\dot{a}}
)
of

\hat{K}_{\circ}
let us consider a smallest stratum in its boundary
\alpha
of the same type,
\beta\neq\alpha
, this means that
\alpha
in inside
the closure of
\beta
:

\alpha\subset Cl(\beta)\subset Cl(\hat{K}^{[r-s,i]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s}))
.

Prescribed coordinate system

\Omega(\beta)
on the stratum

\beta\subset\hat{K}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s})
is restricted to the elementary stratum
\alpha\subset Cl(\beta)
inside its boundary. We shall write:
\alpha\prec\beta
.
In the case when the restriction of a prescribed coordinate system
\Omega(\beta)|_{\alpha}
coincide with the prescribed coordinate system
\Omega(\alpha)

on the smallest stratum, we shall call that the pair of strata

(\alpha,\beta)
is admissible. In the opposite case, the pair of strata
(\alpha,\beta)
is not admissible.

In the case a pair

(\alpha,\beta)
is not admissible, the transformation
\Omega(\beta)|_{\alpha}
into
\Omega(\alpha)
are described below for all types of strata.

For pair of strata of the type
{\bf I}_{{\bf b}}
a non-admissible of
the pair means that the system of coordinates

\Omega(\beta)|_{\alpha}
are related with
\Omega(\alpha)

by one of the following transformations:

\displaystyle(\check{x}_{1},\check{x}_{2})\mapsto(\check{x}_{2},\check{x}_{1}),

(180)

\displaystyle(\check{x}_{1},\check{x}_{2})\mapsto(-\check{x}_{2},-\check{x}_{1}),

(181)

\displaystyle(\check{x}_{1},\check{x}_{2})\mapsto(-\check{x}_{1},\check{x}_{2}),

(182)

\displaystyle(\check{x}_{1},\check{x}_{2})\mapsto(\check{x}_{1},-\check{x}_{2}).

(183)

7.3
An additional structure on the polyhedron
K_{\circ}
of the mapping
c_{0}
(
165
)

7.3.1
An involution
\tau_{c}

An elementary stratum, except the anti-diagonal stratum, is a 2-sheeted covering with respect to a free involution, denoted by

\tau_{c}
(see the formula below)
over the corresponding elementary stratum
of a polyhedron
\hat{K}_{\circ}=K_{\circ}/\tau_{c}
,
a stratum of this polyhedron is denoted by

\displaystyle\hat{K}^{[r-s]}(k_{1},\dots,k_{s}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq r.

(184)

Define the involution
\tau_{c}
on the space

K_{\circ}
by the formula:

\tau_{c}:([x,y],\lambda)\mapsto([ix,iy],\lambda).

This involution is free outside the antidiagonal (recall that by the definition of
K_{\circ}
the antidiagonal is removed, see (
8.3.3
)).
This statement is followed from the coordinate description of elementary strata of

K_{\circ}
, an elementary strata is invariant by the involution
\tau_{c}
and the quotient is well defined.

Lemma 32
.

There exists a mapping

\eta_{c}
, which is included into the following diagram, where
{\bf D}\subset C
is a central quadratic extension by the element
c\in C\setminus{\bf D}
,
c^{2}=b^{2}\in{\bf D}

of the order
4
, vertical mappings are 2-sheeted coverings:

\textstyle{K_{\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{B{\bf D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hat{K}_{\circ}=K_{\circ}/\tau_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{c}}
\textstyle{BC.}

Доказательство.

In each elementary stratum, which consists of points
((x,y),\lambda)
on the sphere,
let us define the action of the dihedral group

{\bf D}=\{a,b:[a,b]=b^{2};b^{4}=e\}
,
by the formulas:

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\colon((x,y),\lambda)\mapsto((y,x),\lambda)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\colon((x,y),\lambda)\mapsto((y,-x),\lambda).

Define the group

C
as the quadratic central extension of the group
{\bf D}
by the element
c
,
where
c^{2}=b^{2}
. Define the action of
C
, generated by elements
a,b,c
,
by the formulas:

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\colon((x,y),\lambda)\mapsto((y,x),\lambda)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\colon((x,y),\lambda)\mapsto((y,-x),\lambda)

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\colon((x,y),\lambda)\mapsto((ix,iy),\lambda).

This action is free and one may define
\hat{K}_{\circ}=K_{\circ}/\tau_{c}
.
The involution

\tau_{c}
, defined above, are given by the action on the element
c\in C\setminus{\bf D}
.
∎

All the statements above are formulated for elementary strata of the polyhedron

\hat{K}_{\circ}=K_{\circ}/\tau_{c}
.
Elementary strata of this polyhedrons (
184
) are divided into the following types:
{\bf I}_{b,c},{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c},{\bf I}_{d,{\bf c}}
, respectively to the type of the subgroup in
C
. Surprisingly, strata of the type
{\bf I}_{b,c}
,

{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c}
are homeomorphic, see Proposition
35
.
(A homeomorphism in prescribed coordinate systems is given by the formula:

\Phi:([x,y],\lambda)\mapsto([ix,y],\lambda)
.
Homeomorphism

\Phi
induces an isomorphism of the corresponding structuring subgroups
{\bf I}_{b,c}\mapsto{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},{\bf c}}
, which is given on the generators by the formula:
b\mapsto ac
,

c\mapsto c
.)

Analogously to (
176
),(
177
),(
178
)
let us define the following open subpolyhedrons:

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{b,c};\circ}/\tau_{c}=\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b,c};\circ}\subset\hat{K}_{\circ},

(185)

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c};\circ}/\tau_{c}=\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c};\circ}\subset\hat{K}_{\circ},

(186)

\displaystyle K_{{\bf I}_{d,c};\circ}/\tau_{c}=\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{d};\circ}\subset\hat{K}_{\circ}.

(187)

The polyhedra

(\ref{Ibc})
,
(\ref{Iac})
are homeomorphic.

Definition of prescribed coordinate system and admissible boundary strata for the subpolyhedrons (
185
), (
186
) is analogous to (
176
), (
177
).

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(a)\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}(c\dot{a})\rtimes_{\theta_{\mathrm{Re}}\,}{\mathbb{Z}}(\dot{A})
,
then we get the following Cartesian square:

\textstyle{BC_{\mathrm{Re}}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B{\mathbb{Z}}(\dot{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{A})}

(196)

The following theorem is a direct analog of Theorem
33
for the group
C_{Re}
.

Theorem 34
.

There exists a mapping (a rezolution)
of
\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}=K_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c};\circ}/\tau_{c}
of the bottom left space of the diagram (
195
) into
the space
BC_{Re}
in the upper left vertex of the diagram (
196
), such that
this mapping commutes with the left vertical projection
BC_{Re}\longrightarrow BC
in the diagram.

Let us prove that Theorem
34
as a corollary of Theorem
33
, let us use the following proposition.

Proposition 35
.

The mapping
\Phi:\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b}\circ}=K_{{\bf I}_{b,c}\circ}/\tau_{c}\to\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}=K_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c};\circ}/\tau_{c}
between the upper left polyhedron in the diagrams (
194
), (
196
),
which is given by the formula
\Phi:([x,y],\lambda)\mapsto([ix,y],\lambda)
, is a homeomorphism.

Доказательство.

A correctness of the definition of
\Phi
and existence of an inverse mapping is straightforward exercise. It is convenient
to note that the formula for

\Phi
gives a well defined mapping only after a factorization by
\tau_{c}
.
∎

7.3.3
Proof of Theorem
34

By Theorem
33
we have the mapping

\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}\to B{\mathbb{Z}}(\dot{A})=S^{1},

(197)

which is defined by the resolution.

Thus, we get a mapping:

\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}\to S^{1},

(198)

which is given by the composition:

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a},c};\circ}/\tau_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi^{-1}}}{{\to}}\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b,c};\circ}/\tau_{c}\to S^{1}.

(199)

We have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{BC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{c}}
\textstyle{BC}

(200)

where the mapping
\Psi
is induced by the corresponding automorphism of the group
C
, which will be denoted by the same:

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle\colon C\to C

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle\colon a\mapsto bc

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle\colon b\mapsto ac

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle\colon c\mapsto c

Then there exist a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{-1}}
\textstyle{\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B{\mathbb{Z}}(\hat{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi^{-1}}
\textstyle{BC\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{A})}

(201)

The kernel of the composition of the bottom mappings
coincides with

\Psi^{-1}(C_{Im})
, this kernel is generated by elements:

c
and
ac^{3}
, this can be represented as a semi-direct product
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}(c)\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{a})
. The group

C
itself is represented by a semi-direct product:

{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}(c)\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{a})\rtimes_{\theta_{\mathrm{Re}}\,}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{A})
, where the action of the generator
\dot{A}
on

{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}(c)\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{a})
is well defined by the formula:
(x,y)\mapsto(x+2y,y)
.
This follows from the formula:

\displaystyle\dot{A}\cdot c=c\cdot\dot{A}

(202)

\displaystyle\dot{A}\cdot\dot{a}=\dot{a}\cdot c^{2}\cdot\dot{A}

(203)

Therefore the classifying mapping

\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{a\times\dot{a}};\circ}\to BC
is lifted into
BC_{\mathrm{Re}}\,=B{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}(c)\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}(\dot{a})\rtimes_{\theta_{\mathrm{Re}}\,}{\mathbb{Z}}(\dot{A})
.
Proposition
35
is proved. ∎

7.5
Construction of equivariant morphism of a bundle over
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}
with layers non-ordered pairs of oriented real line into a bundle with layers
{\mathbb{C}}

Let us divide the space

\hat{K}_{b^{2}}
into two spaces
\hat{K}_{b^{2},R}
,
\hat{K}_{b^{2},L}
with the common
boundary:

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{b^{2}}=\hat{K}_{b^{2},R}\cup_{\Sigma}\hat{K}_{b^{2},L}.

(218)

This common boundary let us denote by

\hat{K}_{b^{2},R}\cap\hat{K}_{b^{2},L}=\Sigma
. Define the subspaces in all elementary strata separately. Let us consider the momenta coordinate

\lambda
, which takes value in the simplex

\Delta^{r-j}
, where
j
is the deep of the strata. Let us consider two faces of simplex
\Delta^{r-j}
, which are defined by momenta, corresponded to angles with positive and negative imaginary residues. The simplex itself is the join of
this two faces, let us assume that a parameter of the join
t\in[0,1]

takes the value
0
on the negative face and the value
1
to the positive face.

Let as call a simplex
\Delta^{r-j}
regular, if numberes of positive and negative coordinates are different. In the case the numberes of positive and negative coordinates coincide, a simplex is called exceptional. In particular, in the case
r-j
is odd, a corresponding simplex is regular. For a regular simplex one may define a type of the simplex. Let us call a simplex is right (a R-type simplex), if the number of positive coordinates is greather then the number of negative coordinates. Let us call a simplex is left (a L-type simplex), if the number of positive coordinates is less then the number of negative coordinates. A type of an exceptional simplex is not defined.

Let us define the decomposition (
218
) for a subpolyhedron
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}^{1}\subset\hat{K}_{b^{2}}
.

Definition 36
.

Let us define the polyhedron
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}^{1}
, which consists of all strata in
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}

of the deep
0
and
1
.
Let us define the following
\tau_{a}
-involutive disjoint decomposition for maximal strata:

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{b^{2}}^{0}=\hat{K}_{b^{2},R}^{0}\cup\hat{K}_{b^{2},L}^{0}.

(219)

We may extend this decomposition the decomposition (
219
) and define the
\tau_{a}
-involutive polyhedrons:

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{b^{2}}^{1}=\hat{K}_{b^{2},R}^{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\hat{K}_{b^{2},L}^{1}.

(220)

In (

) the common boundary polyhedron

.
Obviously, one may define the decomposition (

), such that
the decompositions (

Over the space

On the space, the base of the bundles, the involutions

The involution

We will use the involutions on the space (

.
A fiber of

For a

of a line, which will be used in the formulas,

of lines is associated with inner right-prescribed (left-prescribed) angles in a

Geometrically, when we pass by a path from a maximal stratum

The result of the section is the following.

Theorem 37
.

There exist

(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})
-equivariant mapping
F

of the bundle

into the bundle

, which is fibrewized monomorphism.

Доказательство.

The mapping

over centers of maximal simplexes, where the total collection of residues are defined.
Over each maximal simplex in

this mapping is
denote by

and cross a codimension 1 face, or which join pairs of points
of

This extension is denoted by

Additionally, to conclude the proof, one has to check that obstructions for equivariant extension of the mapping

on
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}^{(1)}
to the hole complex

is trivial.

Lemma 38
.

1. There exists a

-equivariant mapping

on simplexes of the complex in

Total

-resedue

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}_{\alpha_{1}}=\overline{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}\delta_{j}|_{\tau_{a}(\alpha_{2})}}=\prod\limits_{j=1}^{r}\delta_{j}|_{\alpha_{2}}=\theta_{\alpha_{2}},\quad\alpha_{1}=\tau_{a}(\alpha_{2}).

7.5.2
An interpolation of the morphism
F^{(0)}
between pairs of maximal strata of a different type

Let us, briefly, formulate an interpolation rool of the constructed morphism on segments, which join
two strata of a different type. This is the main point in the construction.

Let us consider a maximal

-stratum

\alpha_{1}
and a neighbouring

-stratum

, take a stratum of the deep

in

.

By an interpolation of the total resedue we assume

(

-\Theta(\alpha_{2})
(

)
along the segment, which joins the two maximal strata on different components

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=\bar{\theta}_{2};\quad\Theta(\alpha_{1})=\Theta(\alpha_{2}),

(235)

To calculate

, which coinsids with the conjugation of the collection

The product

в
\alpha_{2}
coincids with

in

, this proves the formula (

).

The secon formula is followed from (

in

coinsids with the sum

Definition 39
.

Recall,
r\geq 3
,
\frac{n-k+2}{2}=r
,
r\equiv-1\pmod{3}
. Assume the angles, which are corresponded to vertexes of the momenta simplex, are ordered.
Take

\displaystyle\beta\subset K_{b^{2},R}^{(1)}\cap K_{b^{2},L}^{(1)}

(236)

and arbitrary stratum of the deep

. Such a stratum has an odd

of angles with resedues

.
Let us define a positive coorientation

on

by the direction from

-maximal stratum, the maximal strata are in the boundary of

.
The invertion of the coorientation

\beta^{\uparrow}\mapsto\beta^{\downarrow}
corrrsponds to the direction from

-stratum to

-stratum on the stratum
\beta
. Let us define a
\beta
-cooriented disorder
\omega(\beta^{\uparrow})\in\{0,1\}
as a number

of transpositions of numbers of different resedues to get the total ordered partition of resedues: the left group of

and the right group of the last

coordinates
with resedues
+{\bf i}
.

When we change the coorientation on
\beta
, the desorder

\omega(\beta^{\downarrow})\in\{0,1\}
is defined as the number
\pmod{2}

of transpositions of coordinates with different resedues to get the collection
of
\frac{r-1}{2}
coordinates with resedues
+{\bf i}
on the left and the collection of

\frac{r-1}{2}
coordinates with resedues
-{\bf i}
on the right. Obviously, we get:

\displaystyle\omega(\beta^{\uparrow})+\omega(\beta^{\downarrow})=1.

(237)

Assume the pair of strata
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
are neighbouring strata in a common strata
\beta
of the deep
1
, which has the desorder

.

for

\displaystyle F^{(0)}\tau_{i}((x,y),t_{+})=F^{(0)}((x,y),-t_{-})=((x,y),-\overline{\theta})

\displaystyle\tau_{i}F^{(0)}((x,y),t_{+})=\tau_{i}((x,y),-\theta t)=((x,y),-\overline{\theta})

\displaystyle F^{(0)}\tau_{a}((x,y),t_{+})=F^{(0)}((y,x),t_{+})=((y,x),-\bar{\theta}t)

\displaystyle\tau_{a}F^{(0)}((x,y),t_{+})=\tau_{a}((x,y),-\theta t)=((y,x),-\bar{\theta}t)

\displaystyle F^{(0)}\tau_{b}((x,y),t_{+})=F^{(0)}((-y,x),t_{+})=((-y,x),-\theta)

\displaystyle\tau_{b}F^{(0)}((x,y),t_{+})=\tau_{b}((x,y),-\theta t)=((-y,x),-\theta)

The third formula for

-strata. The first formula is written for
R
-strata, the case of

-strata is analogous.

Lemma

7.5.4
A morphism of a line bundle
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
-skew-invariant and
\tilde{\tau}_{b}
-invariant into the trivial
{\mathbb{C}}
-bundle over
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}

Let us consider a

(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})
-morphism
F
of the bundle

E_{\mathbb{R}}
into the bundle
E_{\mathbb{C}}
, which is constructed in Theorem
37
.
Let us extend this equivariant morphism by a tensor product

(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})\mapsto(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
, using an additional coordinate
w
on the sphere
S^{0}=\{\pm 1\}
.

Define
(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})
-equivariant

S^{0}
-covering over
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}
(a double covering), which is considered as a bundle
E_{S}
over
\hat{K}_{b^{2}}
with the sphere
S^{0}=\{\pm 1\}
in a layer.
Without equivariant structure we get:

E_{S}=\hat{K}_{b^{2}}\times S^{0}
. The involutions are defined by the following formula:

\displaystyle\tau_{a}

\displaystyle:((x,y),w)\mapsto((-x,y),-w)

(241)

\displaystyle\tau_{b}

\displaystyle:((x,y),w)\mapsto((-y,x),w)

(242)

\displaystyle\tau_{i}

\displaystyle:((x,y),w)\mapsto((x,y),-w).

(243)

Involutions
\tau_{b}
,
\tau_{i}
are now defined in an extended bundle
E_{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes E_{S}
,
let us denote the involutions by
\tilde{\tau}_{b}
,
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
correspondingly. The involution

\tau_{a}
is also defined in the extended bundle, let us denote
\tilde{\tau}_{a}=\tau_{i}\circ\tau_{b}\circ\tau_{a}
, more detailed, let us define
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
by the formulas:

\tilde{\tau}_{a}:((x,y),t_{\pm})\mapsto((-x,y),-t_{\mp})\\

\tilde{\tau}_{a}:((x,y),z)\mapsto((-x,y),z)

and by the formula:

\tilde{\tau}_{a}:((x,y),w)\mapsto((x,y),w),

which is followed from (
241
),(79) and from the formula below (
221
).
Let us note that after re-denotation, if
(x,y)\in\hat{K}_{b^{2},R}
, then
(-x,y)\in\hat{K}_{b^{2},L}
, but we use
R
-system and the formula (
221
).

Involutions
(\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
are defined in the bundle
E_{{\mathbb{C}}}

by a natural way. The involutions
\tilde{\tau}_{b}
,
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
on
{\mathbb{C}}
–bundle
are trivial, the involution

\tilde{\tau}_{i}
acts in each layer by the conjugation. The involution
\tau_{a}

invariantly transforms each oriented layer of the line subbundle

t_{+}
in the bundle
E_{\mathbb{R}}
into the oriented layer of another line subbundle

t_{-}
over the corresponding point of the base, by an orienting preserved morphism of the layers.
The same way, the involution

\tilde{\tau}_{a}
preserves the orienting layers of the bundle
E_{\mathbb{R}}
and changes the orientation of the base vectors. Involution in extended bundle allows to define an equivariant morphism
\tilde{F}
, as a natural extension of the equivariant morphism
F
. Thus, we define
a
(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
-equivariant morphism
\tilde{F}
.

In the collection of the involutions only the involution
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
is non-trivial on
E_{S}
and conjugates the complex structure in

E_{{\mathbb{C}}}
. The involution

\tilde{\tau}_{i}
is an exceptional, because this involution changes the
w
-coordinate (the coordinate of the covering).

Let us formulate the result in the following lemma.

Lemma 40
.

Consider the
(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
-equivariant tensor product
E_{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes E_{S}
,
E_{{\mathbb{C}}}\otimes E_{S}
.

1.The formula of
F
is defined

(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
-equivariant morphism

\tilde{F}:E_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes E_{S}\mapsto E_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes E_{S}.

2. The
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-equivariant factormorphism

F^{\downarrow}:E_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b}}E_{S}\mapsto E_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b}}E_{S}

is well defined.

Proof of Lemma
40

This is well-known fact from linear algebra. ∎

7.5.5
A calculation of the factor-bundles

Lemma 41
.

The bundle

S(E_{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b})}E_{S}
is isomorphic to the trivial
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-equivariant bundle

K\times S^{1}\to K
.

Доказательство.

Define the mapping
F_{\mathbb{C}}:S(E_{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})}E_{S}
into
K\times S^{1}

by the formula:

F_{\mathbb{C}}:((x,y),z,w)\mapsto([x,y],c_{w}(z)).

In this formula
z\in{\mathbb{C}},|z|=1,w\in S^{0}=\{\pm 1\}
.
Let us recall that
(x,y)\in K_{b^{2}}
и
(x,y)=(-x,-y)
,

[x,y]\in K
, where
[x,y]=a[x,y]=b[x,y]
.
Additionally, here we have used

c_{w}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}z,&\text{if}w=1;\\
\overline{z},&\text{if}w=-1.\end{array}\right.

It is clear that
c_{w}(z)=c_{-w}(\overline{z})
.

Let us check that
F_{\mathbb{C}}
is well defined. In fact,

\displaystyle\tau_{a}((x,y),z,w)=((y,x),\overline{z},-w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F_{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\mapsto}}([y,x],c_{-w}(\overline{z}))

\displaystyle\tau_{b}((x,y),z,w)=((-y,x),\overline{z},-w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F_{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\mapsto}}([-y,x],c_{-w}(\overline{z}))

\displaystyle\tau_{i}((x,y),z,w)=((x,y),\overline{z},-w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F_{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\mapsto}}([x,y],c_{-w}(\overline{z}))

In the result in all cases we get the same point

([x,y],c_{w}(z))\in K\times S^{1}
.

The inverse mapping
G_{\mathbb{C}}:K\times S^{1}\to S(E_{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})}E_{S}

is given by the formula:

G_{\mathbb{C}}:([x,y],z)\mapsto((x,y),z,1),

where
z\in S^{1}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
. To check that the formula is well defined it has to be checked that
when a representative of the point
([x,y],z)\in K\times S^{1}
is replaced, the point
((x,y),z,1)\in S(E_{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})}E_{S}
remains the same. A gauge
is the following:
([y,x],z)=([-y,x],z)=([x,-y],z)
.
It is easy to check that the points
((y,x),z,1)
,
((-y,x),z,1)
,
((x,-y),z,1)
coincide to
the point

((x,y),z,1)\in S(E_{\mathbb{C}})\otimes_{(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})}E_{S}
.

Additionally, it is evident that
F_{\mathbb{C}}
and
G_{\mathbb{C}}
are commuted with the projections onto the base
K
and that this morphisms are inversed.
∎

Lemma 42
.

The bundle
S(E_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes_{(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})}E_{S}
isomorphic to the wedge covering
K_{b}\to K
.

Доказательство.

The bundle

S(E_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes_{(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})}E_{S}
after a gauge
(\tau_{a},\tau_{b},\tau_{i})\mapsto(\tilde{\tau}_{a},\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
is splitted into a
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
equivariant wedge
(\tilde{\tau}_{b},\tilde{\tau}_{i})
of two
\tilde{\tau}_{i}
-conjugated non-trivial
\tilde{\tau}_{a}
-equivariant line bundles.
∎

7.5.6
The convolution of the tensor product

By Lemma

and Lemma

, Statement 2 the factormorphism

.

Extension of the morphism (

) over

and over
\hat{K}

) and let us prove that this morphism

,

,

by

. This case is not the only possible, but in the last cases, when the number of corrdinates with the resedues of the type

In the case the number of angles with the resedue

Tipes

of maximal strata near the stratum

-resedues.
With our denotation the stratum

,

equal to

-{\bf i}

.

In this case the resedues on the maximal stratum

,

on

.

The following formula is satisfied:

\displaystyle\omega(\beta_{-{\bf i},0}^{\downarrow})=\omega(\beta_{0,-{\bf i}}^{\uparrow})+1.

(246)

Becuse exceptional numbers are neighbouring, the ordered collection of regular resedues for the strata
\beta_{-{\bf i},0}
,
\beta_{0,-{\bf i}}
coincide. Because on the boundary loop

\displaystyle(+{\bf i},+{\bf i})\mapsto(+{\bf i},-{\bf i})\mapsto(-{\bf i},-{\bf i})\mapsto(-{\bf i},+{\bf i})\mapsto(+{\bf i},+{\bf i})

(247)

of

the stratum

, and

, the calculation of the desorders are based on the common two ordered collections of resedues and the formula (

).

,

7.6
Additional properties of the resolution (

)

Recall,
dim(\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ})=n-k+1,\quad n-k+1\equiv 1\pmod{2}
.
The boundary of the polyhedron
\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}
consists of points on all regular strata, which are closed to antidiagonal (
8.3.3
).
Let us consider of the open polyhedron,
a thin regular neighborhood of the boundary of
\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}
, which is denoted by

\hat{U}_{\circ}\subset\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}
. Analogously, define

.

Consider the mapping

, which is the restriction of
the mapping

, the quotient of the equivariant mapping (

). Because the structuring group

is reduced to the subgroup
(b,c)\subset C
,
the mapping

\hat{U}_{\circ}\to S^{1}
contains values in the even index 2 subgroup
2[\pi_{1}(S^{1})]\subset\pi_{1}(S^{1})
.

Lemma 44
.

1. There exists a subpolyhedron

\hat{U}_{reg,\circ}\subset\hat{U}_{\circ}
, such that the restriction
of the mapping
\hat{\pi}
to the complement

\hat{U}_{\circ}\setminus\hat{U}_{reg,\circ}
is homotopic to the mapping into a point, and the polyhedron itself admits the following mappings:

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{reg,\circ}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\eta}_{reg}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\hat{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{\zeta}_{reg}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\{b,c\},1),

(249)

where

\hat{P}
is a polyhedron of the dimension
\frac{(3n-3k+6)}{4}
(assuming
k\equiv 0\pmod{4}
)
(i.e. of the dimension a little greatеr then

\frac{3}{4}dim(\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ})=\frac{3(n-k+1)}{4}
);
\hat{\eta}_{reg}
,
\hat{\zeta}_{reg}
are mappings,
which are called the control mapping and the structuring mapping, correspondingly, for

.

2. The covering subpolyhedron

U_{reg,\circ}\subset U_{\circ}
, such that the restriction
of the mapping
\pi
to the complement

is homotopic to the mapping into a point, and the polyhedron itself admits the following mappings:

\displaystyle U_{reg,\circ}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{reg}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta_{reg}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\{b\},1),

(250)

where

P
is a polyhedron of the dimension
\frac{n-k+2}{2}
, i.e. of the dimension a little greatеr then

\frac{1}{2}dim(K_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ})=\frac{n-k+1}{2}
,
\eta_{reg}
,
\zeta_{reg}
are mappings,
which are called the control mapping and the structuring mapping, correspondingly, for
P
.

Proof of Lemma
44

Proof of Statement 1. Consider in the polyhedron

\hat{U}_{reg,\circ}\subset\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}
: the polyhedron of elementary strata with prescribes coordinate system,
see susbsubsection (
7.2.7
). Let us define a "nice"coordinate system on each stratum of the polyhedron. A "nice"coordinate system is a coordinate system, which is attached to the anti-diagonal boundary, in which coordinates with residues
-{\bf i}
are singular and associated momenta are small, and coordinates with residue
+{\bf i}
are regular and associated momenta is defined a hyperface of the corresponding hyperface of the
\lambda
-simplex.

The prescribed coordinate system on an elementary stratum could be different with respect to the "nice"coordinate system. Such a stratum let us call regular.
Let us consider all strata for which the "nice"coordinate system coincides with prescribed coordinate system. This implies that for a regular stratum the number of "nice"residue
+{\bf i}
less or equals to the number of "nice"angles with the residue

-{\bf i}
. Denote the polyhedron of all "nice"strata by
\hat{Q}_{nice}\subset\hat{U}_{reg,\circ}
Let us define a
l
-polyhedron, denoted by
(\hat{Q}^{(l)}\cap\Delta_{antidiag})\subset\Delta_{antidiag}
, which is defined as the closure of all maximal strata in
\hat{U}_{reg,\circ}
, with the antidiagonal boundary
a full subpolyhedron strata of the deep
l
. Obviously,

\displaystyle\bigcup_{l,l\leq\frac{n-k+2}{4}}(\hat{Q}^{(l)}\cap\Delta_{antidiag})\supset(\hat{Q}_{nice}\cap\Delta_{antidiag}).

(251)

Each polyhedron in (
251
) admits a mapping onto a polyhedron of the dimension
\frac{3n-3k+6}{4}
. By this map
\frac{n-k-2}{4}
momenta coordinates with
the residue
-{\bf i}
are omitted, but angels with resedue
-{\bf i}
are not ommited. Momenta in
\hat{Q}^{(l)}
are possible only for angles coordinates with the residue
+{\bf i}
.

The mapping

\hat{\pi}
(
248
), restricted on

\hat{K}_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}\setminus\hat{Q}_{nice}
, is homotopic to constant mapping, because
maximal strata have only admissible boundaries. Lemma
44
Statement 1 is proved.

Proof Statement 2. Consider in the polyhedron

U_{reg,\circ}\subset K_{{\bf I}_{b};\circ}
elementary strata with prescribes coordinate system,
see Susbsubsection (
7.2.7
). Let us define a "nice"coordinate system on each stratum of the polyhedron as in Statement 1.
Let us consider all maximal strata for which the "nice"coordinate system coincides with prescribed coordinate system. This implies that for a regular stratum the number of "nice"residue
+{\bf i}
less or equals to the number of "nice"angles with the residue

-{\bf i}
in the full collection of resedues. For a regular stratum the number of "nice"residue
+{\bf i}
less, or equals to the number of all coordinates of the stratum.

Denote by
P\subset\Delta_{antidiag}
the subpolyhedron, consists of boundaries of all regular strata.
The polyhedron
P
is equipped by the projection
U_{reg;\circ}\longrightarrow P
as a projection, which keeps all angles with the residue
+{\bf i}
and all associated momenta.
By definition

is a subpolyhedron in

. This implies that the structured mapping

\zeta_{reg}
exists.

The covering
\pi
over the mapping

\hat{\pi}
(
248
), restricted on

U_{reg,\circ}\setminus\hat{Q}_{nice}
, is homotopic to constant mapping, because
maximal strata have only admissible boundaries.
Lemma
44
Statement 2 is proved.
∎

Lemma 46
.

1. The Hurewich image of the fundamental class of the polyhedrons

in the group

(

Proof of Lemma
46

Proof of Statement 1. The polyhedron
{\bf N}_{{\bf b}}
is decomposed into a collection of maximal strata, each stratum is a manifold and contains the fundamental class. The structure of maximal strata
is agree to the structure of the polyhedron
K
. In
K
there exists the only anti-diagonal stratum (with residues
{\bf i}
) and some number of maximal strata with a mixed structure of the imaginary residues.

It is sufficiently to consider the transfer of the structuring mapping
\eta
over each stratum separately and calculate the image the the fundamental class by the transfer

\eta_{A\times\dot{A}}^{!}
. For the antidiagonal maximal strata of the polyhedron
{\bf N}_{{\bf b};\circ}
we get that the image of the fundamental cycle in

by the projection into

is translated into the fundamental class in

,
k\equiv 0\pmod{2}
. The generic elementary stratum
(which is not the antidiagonal maximal stratum) is the double covering
over the corresponding stratum of the polyhedron
\hat{K}_{b}
.
The transfer with respect to the subgroup

(A,\dot{A})\subset{\bf D}
is shrieked into the covering in the tower of the subgroups
(A,\dot{A})\subset C\to{\bf D}
, therefore the fundamental; class of the stratum is mapped by
p_{A\times\dot{A}\to A}\circ\eta^{!}_{A,\dot{A}}
in

H_{n-k}(K(A,1);{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
to the trivial homology class.

Statement 2 is proved analogously. Lemma
46
is proved. ∎

8
Classifying space for iterated self-intersections of
{\bf I}_{\bf a\times\dot{a}}
-framed immersions

8.1
Preliminary constructions and definitions

Let
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
be two odd positive integers,
n_{1}+n_{2}=n
,

k_{1},k_{2}
be two even positive integers,
n_{1}>k_{1}
,
n_{2}>k_{2}
.
Consider the Cartesian product

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}G=c_{0,1}\times c_{0,2}:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}\to\\
S^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times S^{n_{2}-k_{2}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{2}}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\\
\end{array}

of two mappings (
165
) in the corresponding dimensions.
Let us denote by

I_{1}\times I_{2}
the Cartesian product of the involutions by the
{\bf i}
-multiplication on the factors:

I_{1}:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}};\quad I_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}

on the space

{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}
; the involutions commute. The involution on
{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}
, which permutes the factors, is denoted by
T^{int}
.
This involution is free outside the diagonal. We use an analogous denotation for the target
{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{2}}
.

By the pair of involutions
(I_{1},I_{2})
define another pair of involutions
(\tilde{I},\hat{I})
.
The first involution
\tilde{I}=diag(I_{1},I_{2})
in the pair is the diagonal involution.
Denote
\hat{I}=I_{1}\pmod{\tilde{I}}
. Obviously,
I_{1}\pmod{\tilde{I}}=I_{2}\pmod{\tilde{I}}
and one may consider the involution
\hat{I}
as an involution on the factorspace of
\tilde{I}
-involution. The pair of the involution
(\tilde{I},\hat{I})
, where
\hat{I}
is a bottom (secondary) involution,
\tilde{I}
in a top (primary) involution, depends not of an order of the Cartesian coordinate and describes the same factor. The mapping (
8.1
) is the
\tilde{I}
-double covering over the following mapping:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\tilde{G}:({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})/\tilde{I}\to\\
S^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times S^{n_{2}-k_{2}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{2}}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}.\\
\end{array}

The mapping (
8.1
) is the
\hat{I}
-double covering over the following mapping:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{G}:(({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})/\tilde{I})/\hat{I}\to\\
S^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times S^{n_{2}-k_{2}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{2}}={\mathbb{R}}^{n},\\
\end{array}

where the involution in the target in the diagrames (
8.1
), (
8.1
) are the identity.

Because

(({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})/\bar{I})/\hat{I}\cong S^{n_{1}-k_{1}}/{\bf i}\times S^{n_{2}-k_{2}}/{\bf i}

is the product of the two
{\mathbb{Z}}/4
-lenses, the mapping (
8.1
) is rewritted in the following more clear form:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{G}:S^{n_{1}-k_{1}}/{\bf i}\times S^{n_{2}-k_{2}}/{\bf i}\to\\
S^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times S^{n_{2}-k_{2}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n_{2}}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}.\\
\end{array}

8.1.1
Configuration space

Recall,
\Gamma_{\circ}(X)=(X\times X\setminus diag(X))/T_{X}
,
T_{X}
is the involution, the premutation of the coordinated in the Cartesian product, see (
167
) for a particular case

X={\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n-k}
. We consider the case
X={\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}
and denote
by
\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})
the canonical
2
-sheeted covering over the delated product
\Gamma_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})
.
There is a
(T_{{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}};T_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
-equivariant mapping:

\bar{G}^{(2)}:\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})\to\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),

which is a formal extension of the mapping
G
(
8.1
).

In the more detailed form this mapping looks as following:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\bar{G}^{(2)}:(({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})\times({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}))\setminus diag\\
\to(({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\times({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}))\setminus diag.\end{array}

The involutions
(T_{{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}}};T_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
permute the factors in the source delated product an in the target.
The involution on the 2-sheeted covering

\Gamma_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})\to\Gamma_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})

is denoted by
T
for short; the analogous denotation for the target. The factormapping
\bar{G}^{(2)}/T
is denoted by
G^{(2)}
.
On the delated product
\bar{\Gamma}_{\circ}({\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{1}-k_{1}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\!{\rm P}^{n_{2}-k_{2}})
, equipped with the involution
T
, the involutions
(I_{1},I_{2},T^{int})
defined above on the space (
8.1
),
are extended to the corresponding involutions on the delated product.
An involution and its corresponding extension are denoted by the same for short.
Additionally, the pair of involutions
(\bar{I},\hat{I})
using

Iterated self-intersection polyhedron of

On the secondary delated product

G({\bf x}_{1}\times{\bf y}_{1})=G({\bf x}_{2}\times{\bf y}_{2})\}.\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\overline{{\bf NN}}_{\circ}=\\

Of course, the primery conditions (

8.1.3
Iterated self-intersection polyhedron of

(

The coordinate description is the following. Using the formula (

), let us consider all ordered quadruples in

:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}(({\bf x}_{1,1},{\bf x}_{1,2}),({\bf y}_{1,1},{\bf y}_{1,2});({\bf x}_{2,1},{\bf x}_{2,2}),({\bf y}_{2,1},{\bf y}_{2,2}));\\
